CIVIL LAW 
 
 
I. EFFECT AND APPLICATION OF LAWS 
 
 
 
A. WHEN LAWS TAKE EFFECT – NCC, 
ART. 2 
   
Laws shall take effect after 
fifteen days following the 
completion 
of 
their 
publication 
either 
in 
the 
Official Gazette or in a 
newspaper 
of 
general 
circulation in the Philippines unless it is 
otherwise provided. (Civil Code, Art. 2)  
 
The clause "unless it is otherwise provided" 
refers to the date of effectivity (i.e., as to 
when a law shall take effect) and not to the 
requirement of the publication itself, which 
cannot, in any event, be omitted. (Tanada v.  
Tuvera, G.R. No. L-63915, 1986).  
 
Rules on Effectivity 
 
a) Date is specified in the law - Upon the 
lapse of the said period following its 
complete publication and not before. 
 
b) Date is not specified in the law – Only 
after the fifteen-day period following its 
complete publication, which may either 
be on the 15th or the 16th day depending 
on the language used by the Congress in 
fixing the effectivity date of the statute. 
(Rabuya, 2009) 
 
i. 
15th day – If the law declares that it 
shall become effective “15 days after 
its publication.” 
 
ii. 16th day - If the law declares that it 
shall be effective “after 15 days 
following its publication.” 
 
c) Law provides immediate effectivity or 
upon 
approval 
– 
It 
is 
effective 
immediately after its complete publication 
and NOT after signing by the President. 
 
d) Law is voluminous or published in a 
series – Periods shall be reckoned from 
the release of the last of the series. 
 
Publication Requirement 
 
Publication is INDISPENSABLE in every 
case, but the legislature may at its discretion 
provide that the usual fifteen-day period shall 
be shortened or extended. 
 
What must be published:  
  
Stat-PD-ARR-Chart-MB 
  
1. All statutes, including those of local 
application and private laws.  
 
2. Presidential Decrees and executive 
orders promulgated by the President in 
the exercise of legislative powers.  
 
3. Administrative Rules and Regulations, if 
their purpose is to enforce or implement 
existing 
law 
pursuant 
to 
a 
valid 
delegation.  
 
4. Charter of a city.  
 


<!-- PAGE 11 -->

 
 
2 
5. The circulars issued by the Monetary 
Board, if they are meant not merely to 
interpret but to “fill in the details” of the 
Central Bank Act which that body is 
supposed to enforce.  
 
When publication is NOT necessary:  
  
IRR-LOI 
  
1. Interpretative Rules and Regulations 
and those merely internal in nature. 
 
2. Letters Of Instructions  
  
Note: Municipal ordinances are not covered 
by this rule but by the Local Government 
Code.  
  
Full publication required 
 
The publication must be in full or there is no 
publication at all. The primary purpose of 
publication is to inform the public of the 
contents of the law.  
 
 
B. IGNORANCE OF THE LAW – NCC, ART. 3 
 
Ignorance of the law excuses no one from 
compliance therewith (Civil Code, art. 3). 
 
Presumption of Knowledge of Laws 
 
Every person is presumed to know the law 
even if he has no actual knowledge of the law 
(Tanada v. Tuvera). 
 
The presumption does not apply to foreign 
laws because there is no judicial notice of 
such foreign laws; such laws must be proved 
like any other matter of fact (Ching Huat v. 
Co Heong, G.R. No. L1211, 1947).  
 
 
Ignorance of Fact  
 
Want of knowledge of some fact or facts 
constituting or relating to the subject matter 
at hand. In People v. Caballes (G.R. No. 
203473, December 19, 2012) the Court 
found the accused not guilty of trespass 
because she genuinely believed the LAND, 
she entered was public property, constituting 
a reasonable mistake. 
 
Mistake of Fact vs. Mistake of Law 
 
Mistake of fact 
Mistake of Law 
As to what ignorance pertains to 
Ignorance of some 
fact 
or 
facts 
constituting or relating 
to the subject matter 
on hand. 
Ignorance of the laws 
of the land insofar as 
they apply to the act, 
relation, 
duty, 
or 
matter 
under 
consideration. 
As to nature of mistake 
When 
some 
facts 
which really exist are 
unknown or some fact 
is supposed to exist 
which really does not 
exist 
Occurs 
when 
a 
person 
having 
full 
knowledge 
of 
the 
facts comes to an 
erroneous conclusion 
as to its legal effects. 
Availability of good faith as a defense 
Good 
faith 
is 
an 
excuse. 
Good faith is NOT an 
excuse. 
 
 
C. RETROACTIVITY OF LAWS – NCC, 
ART. 4 
 
Retroactive laws pertain to a legislative act 
that looks backward or contemplates the 
past, affecting acts or facts that existed 
before the act come into effect. (Black’s Law 
Dictionary, 2009) 
 
Retroactive effect of laws 
 
GR: Laws shall have NO retroactive effect, 
unless the contrary is provided.  
  


<!-- PAGE 12 -->

 
 
3 
XPNs:  The following are given retroactive 
effects:  
  
1.Tax laws;  
2. Interpretative statutes;  
3.Laws creating new substantive rights;  
4.Curative statutes;  
5.Remedial laws; 
6. Emergency laws; 
7. When expressly provided; 
8. Penal laws favorable to the accused 
provided, that the accused is not a 
habitual delinquent.  
XPNs to XPNs: No retroactive effect if the 
law: 
 
1. Impairs obligation of contracts; 
2. Is in the nature of ex post facto law or a bill 
of attainder; 
3. Divests vested rights; 
4. Is constitutionally forbidden. 
  
Vested Rights Principle  
 
A vested right is one whose existence, 
effectivity and extent do not depend upon 
events foreign to the will of the holder, or to 
the exercise of which no obstacle exists, and 
which is immediate and perfect in itself and 
not dependent upon a contingency.   
The term "vested right" expresses the 
concept of present fixed interest which, in 
right reason and natural justice, should be 
protected against arbitrary State action, or an 
innately just and imperative right which 
enlightened 
free 
society, 
sensitive 
to 
inherent and irrefragable individual rights, 
cannot deny. (Alexander v. Sps. Escalona, 
G.R. No. 256141. July 19, 2022) 
 
 
 
D. MANDATORY AND PROHIBITORY 
LAWS – NCC, ART. 5 
 
Acts executed against the provisions of 
mandatory or prohibitory laws shall be VOID, 
except when the law itself authorizes their 
validity. (Civil Code, Art. 5) 
 
 
 
Art. 5 refers to mandatory or prohibitory laws, 
as distinguished from those which are merely 
permissive. 
While 
one 
has 
to 
obey 
mandatory statutes, otherwise his acts would 
generally be void, the violation of directory 
laws does not result in invalid acts. (Ramos 
v. Hijos de I. de la Rama, 15 Phil. 554). 
 
Mandatory Laws  
 
These are means to uphold public policy, 
protect the rights of individuals, and maintain 
social order.  
 
In Evangelista v. Santos (G.R. No. L-21438, 
November 27, 1969), the Supreme Court 
emphasized 
the 
principle 
that 
"the 
renunciation of inheritance does not require 
the acceptance of the heirs or the approval 
of the court." The Court further emphasized 
the mandatory nature of the legal provision 
on renunciation, highlighting that certain 
legal rights cannot be waived or modified by 
private agreement.  
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 13 -->

 
 
4 
Prohibitory Laws  
 
It maintains order, protecting public welfare, 
and upholding societal values. In People v. 
Sybico (G.R. No. 198680, July 10, 2013), the 
Supreme Court upheld the anti-graft and 
corrupt 
practices 
statute 
as 
a 
valid 
prohibitory law aimed at protecting public 
interest and promoting good governance. 
 
 
 
 
 
Permissive Laws  
 
Those which may be deviated from, if the 
individual so desires. In the case of "hidden 
treasure," the finder gets 50% and the owner 
of the land on which it is found gets 50%. 
(See Art. 438) However, by agreement, the 
proportion can be changed. (Paras) 
 
 
E. WAIVER – NCC, ART. 6 
 
Right pertains to a legally enforceable claim 
of one person against another, that the other 
shall do a given act, or shall not do a given 
act. (Pineda, 2010) 
 
Waiver is a voluntary and intentional 
relinquishment or abandonment of a known 
legal right or privilege. It has been ruled that 
a waiver to be valid and effective must be 
couched in clear and unequivocal terms 
which leave no doubt as to the intention of a 
party to give up a right or benefit which 
legally pertains to him. (RB Michael Press v. 
Galit, G.R. No. 153510 (2008). 
 
Waiver of rights 
 
Rights may be waived, unless the waiver is 
contrary to law, public order, public policy, 
morals, or good customs, or prejudicial to a 
third person with a right recognized by law.  
(Civil Code, art. 6)  
 
Requisites of a Valid Waiver  
 
1. Existence of a right;  
2. Knowledge of the existence thereof; and  
3. An intention to relinquish the right 
(Herrera v. Borromeo, G.R. No. L-41171, 
1987).  
 
GR: Rights may be waived.  
  
XPN: C-P-E-N  
 
1. If the waiver is Contrary to law, public 
order, public policy, morals or good 
customs;  
2. If the waiver Prejudices a third person;  
3. If the alleged rights do not yet Exist;  
4. If the right is a Natural right.  


<!-- PAGE 14 -->

F. REPEAL AND NULLIFICATION OF 
LAWS – NCC, ART. 7 
 
Laws are repealed only by subsequent ones, 
and their violation or non- observance shall 
not be excused by disuse, or custom or 
practice to the contrary. 
When the courts declare a law to be 
inconsistent with the Constitution, the former 
shall be void and the latter shall govern. 
Administrative or executive acts, orders and 
regulations shall be valid only when they are 
not contrary to the laws or the Constitution. 
(Civil Code, art. 7) 
Effect of Repeal 
 
GR: The repeal of statute defeats all actions 
and proceedings pending under the 
repealed statute at the time of its repeal, 
including those cases which are still 
pending appeal. 
 
XPN: A repealing statute must not interfere 
with vested rights or impair the obligation of 
contracts. (Aisporna vs. CA, 108 SCRA 
481.) 
 
Kinds of Repeal 
a) Express - If the law expressly provides 
for such; and 
b) Implied - If the provisions of the 
subsequent law are incompatible or 
inconsistent with those of the previous 
law, provided, it is impossible to 
reconcile the two laws. 
Requisites of Implied repeal 
(i) When the provisions in the two acts 
on the same subject matter are 
irreconcilably contradictory, in which 
case, the later act, to the extent of 
the conflict, constitutes an implied 
repeal of the earlier one; and 
(ii) When the later act covers the whole 
subject of the earlier one and is 
clearly intended as a substitute; 
thus, it will operate to repeal the 
earlier law. (Lledo v. Lledo, A.M. No. 
P-95-1167, 09 Feb. 2010) 
 
Rules on revival of repealed law 
 
If repealing law is itself repealed by another 
law, the revival of the first law will depend 
on the manner of its repeal: 
 
Manner of repeal 
If the first law is 
expressly repealed 
by the second law, 
and the second law is 
repealed by the third 
law 
If the first law is 
repealed by 
implication by the 
second law and the 
second law is 
repealed by the third 
law 
Effect of repeal of 2nd law 
The first law is NOT 
revived unless 
expressly provided 
so. 
The first law is 
revived 
unless otherwise 
provided 
 
Void Law 
 
A void law is one that lacks enforceability and 
legal effect. In essence, a void law has no 
binding force and is regarded as legally 
inexistent from its inception. It violates 
specific 
constitutional 
or 
statutory 
requirements or goes beyond the authority 
conferred on the law-making body (ex. 


<!-- PAGE 15 -->

 
 
6 
Contrary to law, morals, good customs, 
public order, and public policy). Therefore, 
the law is deemed null and without force or 
effect as if it never existed. 
1. 
OPERATIVE FACT DOCTRINE 
 
The doctrine of operative fact recognizes 
the existence of the law or executive act prior 
to the determination of its unconstitutionality 
as 
an 
operative 
fact 
that 
produced 
consequences that cannot always be 
erased, ignored or disregarded.  
 
In short, it nullifies the void law or executive 
act BUT sustains its effects. It provides an 
exception to the general rule that a void or 
unconstitutional law produces no effect. Its 
use must be subjected to great scrutiny and 
circumspection, and it cannot be invoked to 
validate an unconstitutional law or executive 
act, but is resorted to only as a matter of 
equity and fair play. It applies only to cases 
where extraordinary circumstances exist, 
and 
only 
when 
the 
extraordinary 
circumstances have met the stringent 
conditions that will permit its application. 
 
 
G. JUDICIAL DECISIONS – NCC, ART. 8 
 
Judicial decisions applying or interpreting the 
law, or the Constitution shall form a part of 
the legal system of the Philippines. (Art. 8, 
NCC) 
 
Stare Decisis 
 
The principle of stare decisis is a Latin term 
meaning "to stand by things decided." This 
principle is a cornerstone of judicial decisions 
and jurisprudence in the Philippines, where 
courts are generally bound to follow their 
previous rulings and established doctrines in 
subsequent cases. Stare decisis ensures 
consistency, stability, and predictability in the 
legal system. 
 
 
 
Kinds of Stare Decisis 
 
a. Vertical Stare Decisis. This refers to the 
principle that lower courts are bound to 
follow the rulings of higher courts, 
particularly the Supreme Court. For 
instance, the decisions of the Supreme 
Court must be followed by the Court of 
Appeals, Regional Trial Courts (RTC), 
and lower courts in future cases. 
 
b. Horizontal Stare Decisis. This is the 
idea that the same court (e.g., the 
Supreme 
Court) 
should 
follow 
its 
previous decisions unless there is a 
compelling reason to overturn or modify 
them. 
 
Prospective Application of Doctrines 
 
GR: Prospective Application 
 
As emphasized in People v. Jabinal (G.R. 
No. 
L-30061, 
February 
27, 
1974, 
J. 
Fernando), "judicial decisions interpreting 
the law form part of the legal system, but 
changes 
in 
such 
interpretations 
must 
generally be applied prospectively to avoid 
injustice." 
 
XPN: 
Retroactive 
application 
may 
be 
warranted in certain cases where: 
 
1. The 
new 
doctrine 
is 
merely 
clarificatory. If the decision does not 
overturn a prior rule but instead clarifies 
ambiguities, retroactive application is 
permissible. 
 
Example: In Tanada v. Tuvera (G.R. No. 
63915, April 24, 1985, J. Gutierrez Jr.), 
the 
Supreme 
Court 
clarified 
the 
requirement for publication of laws, 
retroactively 
applying 
the 
principle 
because the rule was declaratory of an 
existing statutory mandate. 
 
2. The doctrine is favorable to the 
accused. Pursuant to Article 22 of the 
Revised 
Penal 
Code, 
penal 
laws 
favorable to the accused are applied 


<!-- PAGE 16 -->

 
 
7 
retroactively unless the accused is a 
habitual delinquent. The same principle 
applies to jurisprudential doctrines that 
mitigate penalties or decriminalize acts. 
 
3. It is required by substantive due 
process 
or 
equity. 
Retroactive 
application may be allowed where failing 
to do so would lead to inequity or the 
denial of substantive rights. 
 
 
H. LEGAL PERIODS – NCC, ART. 13; 
REVISED ADMINISTRATIVE CODE OF 
1987, BOOK I, CHAPTER VIII, SEC. 31 
 
“Year" shall be understood to be twelve 
calendar months 
 
"Month" of thirty days, unless it refers to a 
specific calendar month in which case it shall 
be computed according to the number of 
days the specific month contains; 
 
"Day," to a day of twenty- four hours; and 
 
"Night," from sunset to sunrise. 
(Civil Code, art. 13, as amended by Book I, 
chapter 8, sec. 31 of the Administrative Code 
of 1987 (E.O. No. 292)) 
 
Rule if the last day is a weekend or a legal 
holiday 
 
GR: If the last day of the period, as thus 
computed, falls on a Saturday, a Sunday, or 
a legal holiday in the place where the court 
sits, the time shall not run until the next 
working day [Sec. 1, Rule 22, Rules of 
Court]. 
 
XPN: For contractual obligations, the act will 
still be due despite the fact that the last day 
falls on a weekend or a legal holiday. This is 
because obligations arising from contracts 
have the force of law between the contracting 
parties. 
 
Section 31 provides the framework for 
computing periods under administrative 
law. It states: 
 
"Legal periods, unless otherwise specified by 
law, shall be computed by excluding the 
first day and including the last day. If the 
last day falls on a Sunday or a legal holiday, 
the period shall run until the end of the next 
working day." 
 
This rule ensures consistency and uniformity 
in the interpretation of statutory and 
administrative deadlines. In Republic v. 
Court of Appeals (G.R. No. 93805), the 
Supreme Court affirmed that compliance with 
statutory periods is strictly construed, but 
extensions are permitted under equitable 
circumstances when justified. 
 
 
I. TERRITORIALITY OF PENAL LAWS – 
NCC, ART. 14 
 
Penal laws and those of public security and 
safety shall be obligatory upon all who live or 
sojourn in the Philippine territory, subject 
to the principles of public international law 
and to treaty stipulations. (Art. 14, Civil Code) 
 
GR: 
All 
individuals—regardless 
of 
citizenship, race, or social status—are 
equally subject to the Philippine penal 
system for crimes committed within the 
jurisdiction of the Philippines. 
 
XPNs: 
 
1. If treaty stipulations provide the contrary; 
2. lf a law of preferential application provides 
the contrary; and 
3. lf a principle of public international law 
provides the contrary.  
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 17 -->

 
 
8 
Sample of law of preferential application 
 
An example is the congressional privilege of 
speech and debate under Section 11, Article 
VI of the 1987 Philippine Constitution which 
provides 
that 
"no 
Member 
shall 
be 
questioned nor be held liable in any other 
place for any speech or debate in the 
Congress or in any committee thereof." 
 
 
J. CONFLICT OF LAWS – NCC, ARTS. 15-
18 
 
Conflict of Laws, also called Private 
International Law, is that part of the municipal 
laws of a state which directs its courts and 
administrative agencies, when confronted 
with a legal problem involving a foreign 
element, whether or not they should apply a 
foreign law or foreign laws (Paras, Philippine 
Conflict of Laws, 1996, p. 2). 
 
Functions of Conflict of Law Rules  
  
1. To proscribe the conditions under which 
a court or agency is competent to 
entertain a suit or proceeding involving 
facts containing a foreign element;  
2. To determine the extent, validity and 
enforceability of foreign judgment  
3. To determine for each class of cases the 
particular system if law by reference to 
which the rights of the parties must be 
ascertained. 
 
Elements of Conflict of Laws 
 
a. Legal problem or case involving a foreign 
element;  
 
Foreign element refers to the factual 
situation cutting across territorial lines, 
affected by diverse laws of two or more 
states. The presence of a foreign element 
in a case determines the existence of a 
conflict of laws. Where there is no foreign 
element, no conflict of laws exists (Agpalo, 
Conflict of Laws, 2004, p. 12). 
 
b. Determination of whether the law or 
judgments of other state/s will govern, and if 
so, the extent of its recognition/application in 
the forum. 
 
1. Lex Nationalii – Article 15 of the 
Civil Code 
 
Laws relating to family rights and duties, or 
to the status, condition and legal capacity of 
persons are binding upon citizens of the 
Philippines, even though living abroad. 
 
GR: Citizenship is the basis for determining 
the personal law applicable. This rule covers 
family rights and duties, status, condition and 
legal capacity of persons. 
 
XPN: Article 26 of par 2 of the Family Code 
 
 
2. Lex Rei Sitae – Article 16 of the 
Civil Code 
 
GR: Real property as well as personal 
property is subject to the law of the country 
where it is situated (Art. 16 [1], NCC). This 
comprises all aspects including formalities 
(Orayon Savings Bank v. Suzuki, G.R. No. 
205487, November 12, 2014).  
 
XPNs: National law of the decedent whose 
succession is under consideration will 
govern with respect to the following aspects 
of succession, whether testate or intestate: 
 
a. Capacity to succeed  
b. Intrinsic validity of the will 
c. Amount of successional rights  
d. Order of succession 
 


<!-- PAGE 18 -->

 
 
9 
3. Lex Contractus 
 
Lex contractus (also called the “proper law of 
the contract”) is the conflict-of-laws rule that 
the intrinsic/essential validity and substantive 
incidents of a contract are governed by the 
law chosen or intended by the parties, 
expressly or impliedly. It’s application is 
triggered when a contract dispute has foreign 
elements requiring a choice of law. 
 
Lex Contractus is generally applied when the 
issue pertains to the intrinsic validity and 
substantive incidents of a contract. 
No conflicts rule on essential validity of 
contracts is expressly provided for in our 
laws. The rule followed by most legal 
systems, however, is that the intrinsic validity 
of a contract must be governed by the lex 
contractus or "proper law of the contract." 
(Colmenares, et al. v. Duterte, et al., G.R. 
No. 245981, August 9, 2022) 
 
[It can either be] the law voluntarily agreed 
upon by the parties (the lex loci voluntatis) or 
the law intended by them either expressly or 
implicitly (the lex loci intentionis). The law 
selected may be implied from such factors as 
substantial connection with the transaction, 
or the nationality or domicile of the parties. 
Philippine courts would do well to adopt the 
first and most basic rule in most legal 
systems, namely, to allow the parties to 
select the law applicable to their contract, 
subject to the limitation that it is not against 
the law, morals, or public policy of the forum 
and that the chosen law must bear a 
substantive relationship to the transaction. 
(Colmenares, et al. v. Duterte, et al., G.R. 
No. 245981, August 9, 2022) 
 
Example: 
 
Even where contracts stipulate foreign 
governing law and foreign arbitral tribunals, 
the Court may sustain those stipulations 
absent a showing they offend Philippine 
law/morals/public policy—again reflecting lex 
contractus with forum limits. 
 
 
4. Lex Loci Celebrationis- Article 17 
of the Civil Code.  
 
GR: The forms and solemnities of contracts, 
wills, and other public instruments shall be 
governed by the laws of the country in which 
they are executed (Art. 17, NCC). 
 
When a Filipino is in a foreign country, he is 
authorized to make a will in any of the forms 
established by the law of such country (Art. 
815, NCC). 
XPNs: 
 
a. Marriages solemnized involving abroad 
Filipinos when such marriages are void in 
the Philippines, such as: 
 
1. Marriages contracted by minors, even 
with the consent of parents or guardian 
(Art. 35 [1], FC);  
 
2. Bigamous or polygamous marriages 
not falling under Art. 41, FC (Art. 35 [4], 
FC);  
 
3. Marriages contracted through mistake 
of identity of the other contracting party 
(Art. 35 [5], FC);  
 
4. Non-compliance of recording 
requirements with appropriate civil 
registry and registries of properties 
concerning the judgment of annulment 
or of absolute nullity of marriage, the 
partition and distribution of properties of 
spouses, and the delivery of the 
presumptive legitimes of children (Art. 
35 [6], FC);  
 
5. Void marriages by reason of 
psychological incapacity of a party to 
comply with the essential marital 
obligations of marriage (Art. 36, FC);  
 


<!-- PAGE 19 -->

 
 
10 
6. Incestuous marriages (Art. 37, FC); 
and  
 
7. Void marriages for reasons of public 
policy (Art. 38, FC). 
 
Jurisprudential example 
 
Since the marriage was celebrated in Manila, 
the law of the place of celebration (lex loci 
celebrationis) applies. Under Article 26 of the 
Family Code, a marriage that is valid where 
celebrated is valid in the Philippines, except 
in cases expressly prohibited by law. 
Consequently, any issue involving the 
validity or nullity of the marriage is governed 
by Philippine law, which has jurisdiction to 
determine whether grounds exist to declare 
the marriage void or voidable under the 
Family Code. 
 
(★Paul 
Ambrose 
v. 
Louella 
Suque-
Ambrose, G.R. No. 206761, June 23, 2021, 
as penned by J. Gaerlan) 
 
b. Rule of Extra-territoriality. 
 
When the acts referred to are executed 
before the diplomatic or consular officials of 
the Republic of the Philippines in a foreign 
country, the solemnities established by 
Philippine laws shall be observed in their 
execution (Art. 17 [2], NCC). 
 
c. Rule on Prohibitive Laws. 
 
Prohibitive laws concerning persons, their 
acts or property, and those which have for 
their object public order, public policy or good 
customs shall not be rendered ineffective by 
laws or judgments promulgated, or by 
determinations or conventions agreed upon 
in a foreign country (Art. 17[3], NCC). 
 
Under the rule of lex loci celebrationis, a 
contract executed in a foreign country is 
considered valid as long as it is valid in 
accordance with the law of the place where it 
was celebrated. However, if such contract is 
prohibited under Philippine laws like those 
provided under Art. 26 par. [1] of E.O. 207, 
then such contract is not valid.

5. Lex Domicilii 
 
Lex domicilii is the conflicts rule that a 
person’s domicile (legal residence) supplies 
the governing law for matters at issue. 
 
Domicile v. Residence 
 
There is a difference between domicile and 
residence. Residence is used to indicate a 
place of abode, whether permanent or 
temporary; 
domicile 
denotes 
a 
fixed 
permanent residence to which when absent, 
one has the intention of returning. Chen v. 
Calasan, et al., G.R. No. 161685, July 24, 
2007 
 
However, residence for election purposes is 
used 
synonymously 
with 
domicile. 
Macalintal v. COMELEC, G.R. No. 157013, 
July 10, 20223 
 
Application of Lex Domicilii 
 
It is applicable when he governing choice-of-
law rule makes domicile the connecting 
factor. For example, the contract provides 
that the law of the country where the contract 
is executed shall govern; but the law of that 
country points to the law of the domicile of 
the parties. In such case, Lex Domicilii shall 
govern. 
 
 
6. Lex Fori 
 
Lex fori means the law of the forum, or the 
law of the country where the case is filed and 
tried. It is generally applicable where the 
issue pertains to procedural matters. In other 
words, generally, the procedural law of the 
country where the case is filed shall govern. 
 
The lex fori, the law of the forum, is 
particularly important because, as we have 
seen earlier, matters of ‘procedure’ not going 
to the substance of the claim involved are 
governed by it; and because the lex fori 
applies whenever the content of the 
otherwise applicable foreign law is excluded 
from application in a given case for the 
reason that it falls under one of the 
exceptions to the applications of foreign law. 
(Standard Chartered Bank v. Philippine 
Investment Two (SPV-AMC), Inc., et al.) 
 
 
7. Lex Loci Delicti 
 
Lex loci delicti is the traditional conflict-of-
laws rule in torts: the law of the place where 
the tort/wrong was committed governs 
substantive rights and liabilities. 
 
Note: The Court has expressly observed 
criticism of the traditional lex loci delicti rule 
and adopted, in proper cases, the state of 
the 
most 
significant 
relationship 
approach 
to 
reach 
just 
results 
in 
transnational tort disputes. 
 
Jurisprudential example: In Saudi Arabian 
Airlines v. Court of Appeals, et al., G.R. No. 
122191, October 8, 1998, the Supreme Court 
held: 
 
Moreover, with the widespread criticism of 
the 
traditional 
rule 
of lex 
loci 
delicti 
commissi, modern theories and rules on tort 
liability have been advanced to offer fresh 
judicial approaches to arrive at just results. In 
keeping abreast with the modern theories on 
tort liability, we find here an occasion to apply 
the 
"State 
of 
the 
most 
significant 
relationship" rule, which in our view should 
be appropriate to apply now, given the 
factual context of this case. 
 
In applying said principle to determine the 
State which has the most significant 
relationship, the following contacts are to be 
taken into account and evaluated according 
to their relative importance with respect to 
the particular issue: 
 
(a) the place where the injury occurred; 
(b) the place where the conduct causing the 
injury occurred; 


<!-- PAGE 21 -->

 
 
12 
(c) the domicile, residence, nationality, place 
of incorporation and place of business of 
the parties, and 
(d) the place where the relationship, if any, 
between the parties is centered. 
 
As already discussed, there is basis for the 
claim that over-all injury occurred and lodged 
in the Philippines. There is likewise no 
question that private respondent is a resident 
Filipina national, working with petitioner, a 
resident foreign corporation engaged here in 
the business of international air carriage. 
Thus, the "relationship" between the parties 
was centered here, although it should be 
stressed that this suit is not based on mere 
labor law violations. From the record, the 
claim that the Philippines has the most 
significant contact with the matter in this 
dispute, raised by private respondent as 
plaintiff below against defendant (herein 
petitioner), in our view, has been properly 
established. 
 
 
8. Lex Loci Solutionis 
 
Lex loci solutionis is the conflict-of-laws 
principle that looks to the law of the place 
where the contractual obligation is to be 
performed to govern substantive issues tied 
to performance. In other words, when the 
issues involved pertain to manner of 
performance, breach, consequences, or 
such other matters pertaining to performance 
of contractual obligations, the court may 
apply lex loci solutionis, or the law of the 
place where the contractual obligation is to 
be performed. 
 
 
9. Renvoi 
 
Renvoi literally means “to refer back”. 
Renvoi takes place when the conflicts rule of 
the forum makes a reference to a foreign law, 
but the foreign law is found to contain a 
conflict rule that returns or refers the matter 
back to the law of the forum (Remission) 
(Paras, Conflict of Laws, 1996, p. 211) 
 
Double Renvoi 
 
It occurs when the local court, in adopting the 
foreign court theory, discovers that the 
foreign court accepts the renvoi (Paras, 
supra, p. 214). 
 
Doctrine of Processual Presumption 
 
When a foreign law is not pleaded or, even if 
pleaded, is not proved, the presumption is 
that the foreign law is the same is ours. In our 
jurisdiction, courts do not take judicial notice 
of 
foreign 
laws 
(Philippine 
National 
Construction Corporation v. Asiavest, G.R. 
No. 172301, August 19, 2015)

II. PERSONS 
 
 
Person, defined 
 
A person is any being, natural or artificial, 
capable of possessing legal rights and 
obligations (2 Sanchez Roman 110). 
 
 
A. JURIDICAL CAPACITY 
 
Juridical capacity is the fitness to be the 
subject of legal  relations. (Art. 37, NCC).   
 
The union of both juridical capacity and 
capacity to act constitutes full civil 
capacity (2 Sanchez Roman, 112-114). 
 
1. Acquisition 
 
a. Natural Persons 
 
Natural persons are those human beings 
created by God through the intervention of 
the parents. 
 
Commencement of Civil Personality 
 
Birth 
determines 
personality, 
but 
the 
conceived child shall be considered born for 
all purposes that are favorable to it, provided 
it is born later with the conditions specified in 
Art. 41. (Art. 40, NCC) 
 
Note: This provision has been superseded by 
Art. 5 of P.D. 603 (The Child and Youth 
Welfare Code), which declares that the civil 
personality of the child shall commence from 
the time of his conception, for all purposes 
favorable to him, subject to the requirements 
of Art. 41. 
 
Acquisition of personality through birth 
 
GR: Actual or permanent personality – 
Personality begins at birth, not at conception. 
 
XPN: 
Presumptive 
or 
Temporary 
personality - The law considers the 
conceived child as born (conceptus pro nato 
habetur). 
 
The provisional personality of a conceived 
child (conceptus pro nato habetur) under Art. 
40, 
expressly 
limits 
such 
provisional 
personality by imposing the condition that the 
child should be subsequently born alive: 
"Provided it be born later with the condition 
specified in the following article." (Geluz v. 
CA, G.R. No. L- 16439, 20 July 1961) 
 
Meaning of “born later in accordance with 
law” 
 
A fetus with an intra-uterine life of: 
 
1. Less than 7 months – It must survive 
for at least 24 hours after its complete 
delivery from the maternal womb; or 
 
Complete deliver refers to the cutting of 
the umbilical cord from the mother’s 
womb. 
 
2. At least 7 months – If born alive it shall 
be considered born even if it dies within 24 
hours after complete delivery. 
 
Right of the conceived child 
 
Since a conceived child has a provisional 
personality even while inside the mother’s 
womb, it is entitled to the following rights: 
 
1. Right to support; 
2. To be acknowledged; and 
3. To receive donations. (Rabuya, 2009) 
 
End of civil personality 
 
For natural persons, civil personality 
ceases by death. 
 
For juridical persons, civil personality 
ceases by termination of existence.

susceptible

and

Board, G.R. No. L-11973, June 30, 1959).

B. CAPACITY TO ACT 
 
Capacity to act is the power to do acts with 
legal effect. 
 
 
 
Juridical Capacity v. Capacity to Act 
Juridical Capacity

to

1. Presumption of Existence 
 
The presumption of capacity to act is a 
fundamental principle Philippine civil law, 
deeply rooted in the premise that individuals 
are presumed competent to exercise their 
civil rights and fulfill their obligations 
unless proven otherwise. 
 
This presumption is anchored in Article 37 of 
the Civil Code of the Philippines, which 
states that "juridical capacity, which is the 
fitness to be the subject of legal relations, is 
inherent in every natural person and is lost 
only through death."  
 
2. Acquisition – R.A. No. 6809 
 
The acquisition of the capacity to act 
underwent a significant legislative change 
with the passage of Republic Act No. 6809, 
which lowered the age of majority from 21 to 
18 years old. 
 
 
Lowering of the Age of Majority 
 
Section 1 of R.A. No. 6809 amended Article 
234 of the Family Code, redefining the age 
of majority to 18 years old. Upon reaching 
this age, an individual is presumed to have 
full capacity to act unless disqualified by 
other provisions of law. This includes the 
capacity to: 
 
 Enter into contracts; 
 Own and dispose of property; 
 Sue and be sued; and 
 Make independent decisions without 
parental or guardian intervention. 
 
3. Restrictions 
 
Minority, insanity or imbecility, the state 
of being a deaf-mute, prodigality and civil 
interdiction 
are 
mere 
restrictions 
on 
capacity to act, and do not exempt the 
incapacitated 
person 
from 
certain 
obligations, as when the latter arise from his 
acts or from property relations, such as 
easements. (Article 38, NCC)  
(1) Minority (below 18 years old) A minor 
needs parental consent before he can 
enter into an ordinary contract, but a 
contract he entered without parental 
consent is merely voidable (Art. 1327, 
NCC) and will arise to certain obligations 
from him (Cruz v. Court of Appeals, G.R.  
No. L-40880, October 13, 1979).   


<!-- PAGE 25 -->

 
 
16 
 
(2) Insanity (condition of one whose mental 
faculties are diseased) or imbecility (a 
condition of a person who’s advanced in age 
has the mental capacity comparable to that 
of a child between two to seven years of age) 
(Rabuya).   
 
Insanity in one thing does not mean insanity 
in another as in the case of monomania or 
partial insanity (Standard Oil Company of 
New York v. Arenas, G.R. No. L-5921, July 
25, 1911).   
 
An insane person under guardianship who 
entered a contract is presumed to be insane 
and the contract is voidable, (Francisco v.  
Herrera, G.R. No. 139982, November 21, 
2002) but may be rebutted by substantial 
evidence that the same acted within his lucid 
interval, 
thus 
such 
contract 
is 
valid 
(Dumaguin v.  Reynolds, G.R. No. L-3572, 
September 30, 1952).   
 
(3) Deaf-mute   
 
It refers to the state of being deaf-mute does 
not disqualify the ability to give consent. 
Contracts entered into by deaf-mute are 
valid. However, a deaf-mute who does not 
know how to write is declared incapable of 
giving consent therefore rendering the 
contract voidable. 
 
(4) Prodigality  
 
It refers to the state of squandering money or 
property with a morbid desire to prejudice the 
heirs of a person.   
 
NOTE: 
Prodigality 
per 
se 
doesn’t 
automatically modify or restrict a person’s 
capacity to act.  There must be a declaration 
thereof and be placed under guardianship 
under the Rules on Special Proceeding.   
 
 
 
 
 
(5) Civil interdiction   
 
The depriation by the court of a person’s 
right:  
 
i. To have parental or marital authority;   
ii. To be the guardian of the person and 
property of a ward;   
iii. To dispose of his property by an act inter 
vivos (he cannot donate, for this is an act 
inter vivos; but he can make a will, for this 
is a disposition mortis causa); and   
iv. To manage his own property (Art. 34, 
RPC).   
 
The penalty of civil interdiction is given to a 
criminal punished by imprisonment of 12 
years and 1 day or more (Art. 41, RPC).   
 
These restrictions do not exempt the 
incapacitated person from certain obligations 
when the latter arise from his acts or from 
property relations such as easements (Art. 
38, NCC).   
 
a. Further Restrictions Arising from 
Minority – R.A. No. 11596 
 
Republic Act No. 11596, also known as the 
Prohibition of Child Marriage Act, imposes 
additional restrictions on the capacity to act 
of minors, particularly in the context of 
marriage.  
 
The law aims to protect minors by 
categorically prohibiting child marriages and 
addressing related acts or practices. 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 26 -->

 
 
17 
Key Provisions of R.A. No. 11596 
 
Prohibition of Child Marriage 
 
It is unlawful for any person to solemnize, 
facilitate, or arrange child marriages or to 
coerce minors into marriage, whether or not 
a ceremony takes place. 
 
A child marriage is defined as a marriage 
where at least one of the parties is under 
eighteen (18) years of age, regardless of 
circumstances or parental consent. 
 
All marriages involving a minor are deemed 
void ab initio, even if parental consent is 
obtained or the marriage is culturally or 
traditionally sanctioned. 
 
Integration with Other Laws 
 
The restrictions under R.A. No. 11596 
operate alongside existing laws, such as the 
Family Code and Special Protection of 
Children Against Abuse, Exploitation, and 
Discrimination Act (R.A. No. 7610), to 
safeguard the welfare of minors. 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 27 -->

 
 
18 
 
III. FAMILY RELATIONS 
FAMILY CODE OF THE PHILIPPINES (FC) 
– EXECUTIVE ORDER NO. 209, AS 
AMENDED 
 
 
A. MARRIAGE – FC, ARTICLES 1-148; 
1987 CONSTITUTION, ART. II, SEC. 12 
AND ART. XV 
 
1. General Principles 
 
a. State Policy on Marriage 
 
 
A marriage is a special contract of 
permanent union between a man and a 
woman entered into in accordance with law 
for the establishment of conjugal and family 
life. 
 
It is the foundation of the family and an 
inviolable social institution whose nature, 
consequences, and incidents (NCI) are 
governed by law and not subject to 
stipulation, except that marriage settlements 
may fix the property relations during the 
marriage within the limits provided by the 
Code (Art. 1, FC). 
Marriage v. Ordinary Contract

and 
incidents (NCI) are

by 
law 
(except

settlements)

are 
generally fixed by the 
parties

of

for damages.

b. Essential and Formal Requisites of 
Marriage 
 
I. Essential Requisites 
 
The following are the essential requisites of 
marriage: 
 
1. Legal capacity of the contracting parties 
who must be a male and a female; and 
 
2. Consent freely given in the presence of the 
solemnizing officer. (Art. 2, FC) 
 
1) Legal capacity 
 
a. Parties must be a male and a female; 
 
Two females are incapable of entering into 
marriage. 
There 
is 
no 
constitutional 
protection of the rights of marriage between 
two persons of the same sex (Jones v. 
Hallahan, 501 S.W.2d 588, November 9, 
1973). 
 
b. 18 years old and above; and 
 
The attainment of the required minimum age 
should be reckoned, not on the date of filing 
of the application for issuance of a marriage 
license, but on the date of the marriage.  


<!-- PAGE 28 -->

 
 
19 
Pursuant to Article 6 of the Family Code, 
parties may contract marriage on the date of 
the solemnization of the marriage, i.e., when 
they 
appear 
personally 
before 
the 
solemnizing officer and declare in the 
presence of not less than two witnesses of 
legal age that they take each other as 
husband and wife (Rabuya, The Law on 
Persons and Family Relations, 2021). 
  
c. Not subject to any legal impediments in 
Arts. 37 and 38 of Family Code (Art. 5, FC). 
 
 
2) Consent of the contracting parties 
 
Freely given means not vitiated nor 
rendered defective by fraud, violence, force, 
undue influence, intimidation, or mistake 
(FVUIM). 
 
If consent is vitiated with error, fraud, 
intimidation, etc., it is merely voidable (valid 
until 
annulled). 
However, 
if 
there 
is 
absolutely no consent at all, as it was 
intended to be a jest, the marriage is void. 
 
1. Made 
in 
the 
presence 
of 
a 
solemnizing officer; 
 
2. Parties must have the necessary 
consent of parents if contracted marriage 
at the age of 18 years old but below 21 
years old (Art. 14, FC). 
 
II. Formal Requisites 
 
The following are the formal requisites of 
marriage: 
 
1. Authority of solemnizing officer 
2. Valid marriage license 
3. Marriage ceremony 
 
1) Authority of Solemnizing Officer; 
 
GR: Those solemnized by any person not 
legally authorized to perform marriages are 
VOID (Art. 35, par. 2, FC).  
 
XPN: The marriage was contracted with  
either or both parties believing in good faith 
that  the solemnizing officer had the legal 
authority to  do so. If a person seeks to 
impugn the validity of  a marriage on the 
ground that the person who  solemnized it 
was not really authorized, such lack  of 
authority must be proved by the person  
petitioning (De Cardenas vs. Cardenas, et 
al., G.R.  No. L-8218, Dec. 15, 1955). 
 
Persons Authorized to Solemnize 
Marriages: (Art. 7-8, 10, FC)  
 
a. Any incumbent member of the 
judiciary within the court’s jurisdiction  
 
A marriage solemnized by a judge outside 
the court’s jurisdiction will not affect the 
validity of the marriage but may subject the 
officiating official to administrative liability 
(Beso vs. Daguman, A.M. No. 99-1211, 
January 28, 2000). The defect is a mere 
irregularity in a formal requisite (Navarro vs. 
Domagtoy, A.M. No. MTJ-96-1088, July 19, 
1996). 
 
Members of the Judiciary:  
 
1. Metropolitan/Municipal Trial Courts 
(MTC) and Regional Trial Courts 
(RTC) 
Judges 
(within 
their 
territorial jurisdiction)  


<!-- PAGE 29 -->

 
 
20 
 
2. Court of Appeals, Court of Tax 
Appeals, 
Sandiganbayan, 
and 
Supreme 
Court 
Justices 
(anywhere in the Philippines)  
 
Where a judge solemnizes outside his court’s 
jurisdiction, there is a resultant irregularity 
in the formal requisite laid down in Article 3, 
which while it may not affect the validity of the 
marriage, may subject the officiating official 
to 
administrative 
liability 
(Navarro 
v. 
Domagtoy, A.M. No. MTJ-96-1088, July 19, 
1996).  
 
b. Priests, rabbis, ministers of any 
church 
 
Requisites to Perform Marriage:  
1. Must be duly authorized by his or her 
church or religious sect in writing. 
2. Must act within the limits of the written 
authority granted to him or her by the 
church or religious sect. 
3. His written authority must be registered 
with the civil registrar general; and 
4. At least one of the contracting parties 
belongs to the solemnizing officer’s 
church or religious sect (Art. 7 [2], FC).  
 
c. Ship captains or airplane chiefs or 
pilot  
 
Requisites to Perform Marriage:  
 
1. The marriage must be in articulo mortis (at  
least one of the parties is at the point of  
death).   
 
Note: Effect of recovery from illness of 
dying person - Marriage is still valid (Art. 
27, FC);  
 
2. 
The 
marriage 
must 
be 
between 
passengers or crew members; and 
 
3. Generally, the plane must be in flight or 
the ship must be at sea or during 
stopovers at the port of call (Art. 31, FC).  
 
d. Military commanders of a unit; 
 
Requisites to Perform Marriage:  
 
1. He or she must be a commissioned officer, 
or an officer in the armed forces holding 
rank by virtue of a commission from the 
president (his rank should start from a 
second lieutenant, ensign and above). 
2. Assigned chaplain must be absent at the 
time of the marriage. 
3. The marriage must be one in articulo 
mortis; and 
4. The contracting parties, whether members 
of the armed forces or civilians, must be 
within the zone of military operation (Art. 
32, FC).  
 
e. Consul generals, consuls or vice 
consuls  
 
Marriages between Filipino citizens abroad 
may 
be 
solemnized 
by 
them. 
When 
performed abroad, or specifically in his place 
of assignment, the solemnities established 
by Philippine laws shall be observed in their 
execution (Art. 10, NCC).  
 
f. Duly elected mayors of cities and 
municipalities pursuant to the Local 
Government Code.   
 
The mayor of a city or municipality, within 
their exclusive jurisdiction, is empowered to 
solemnize a marriage (Sec. 444 [b](1)(xviii), 
Local Government Code).  
 
When the mayor is temporarily incapacitated 
to perform his duties for physical or legal 
reasons such as, but not limited to, leave of 
absence, travel abroad, any suspension from 


<!-- PAGE 30 -->

 
 
21 
office, the vice mayor or the highest ranking 
Sangguniang 
Bayan 
member 
shall 
automatically exercise the powers and 
perform the duties of the local chief executive 
(Sec. 46, LGC).  
 
They were not included under the Family 
Code but their authority was restored by the 
Local Government Code, which took effect 
on 1 January 1992. Thus, if a marriage was 
celebrated by a mayor after the effectivity of 
the Family Code on 3 August 1988 but prior 
to the effectivity of the Local Government 
Code on 1 January 1992, such marriage is 
void ab initio (Rabuya, The Law on Persons 
and Family Relations, 2006, p. 167).  
 
Duty of the Solemnizing officer:  
 
The solemnizing officer shall state under 
oath that he ascertained the qualifications of 
the contracting parties and found no legal 
impediment to the marriage (Art. 34, FC). 
 
Public Solemnization – based on the 
premise that the State takes an active 
interest in marriage. It may be done in the: 
 
1. Chambers of the judge or in open court;  
2. Church; 
3. Chapel or temple; or 
4. Office of the consul-general, consul or vice 
consul (Estrada vs. Escritor, AM No. P-02-
1651, June 22, 2006).  
 
XPNs to the Public Solemnization:  
 
1. At the point of death (articulo mortis);  
 
2. In a remote place; or  
 
3. When both of the parties requested in  
writing, solemnization in some other place. It  
must be stated in the sworn statement  
(Navarro vs. Judge Dumagtoy, AM No. MTJ-
96- 1088, July 19, 1996). 
 
 
 
 
2) Valid marriage license (except in a 
marriage of exceptional character) 
 
A marriage license is required in order to 
notify the public that two persons are 
about to be united in matrimony and that 
anyone who is aware or has knowledge 
of any impediment to the union of the two 
shall make it known to the local civil 
registrar.  
 
The 
requirement 
and 
issuance 
of 
marriage 
license 
is 
the 
State’s 
demonstration of its involvement and 
participation in every marriage (Rabuya, 
The Law on Persons and Family 
Relations, 2018). 
 
Note: what is required is the marriage 
license, not the marriage certificate. The 
latter is not an essential or formal requisite; 
thus, an oral solemnization is valid. In fact, a 
marriage may be proved by oral evidence 
(Paras, 2016). 
 
Validity 
 
Valid in any part of the Philippines for a 
period of one hundred twenty (120) days 
from date of issue. It is deemed automatically 
cancelled at the expiration of the said period 
(Art. 20, FC). 
 
Jurisprudential example on marriage 
license 
 
The Family Code of the Philippines provides 
that one of the formal requisites of a marriage 
is a valid marriage license except in the 
cases provided by the Code. Article 4 of the 
Code provides that the absence of any of 
the essential or formal requisites shall 
render the marriage void ab initio, except 
as stated in Article 35(2). Article 35 provides 


<!-- PAGE 31 -->

 
 
22 
that marriages solemnized without a license 
shall be void from the beginning except for 
those stated in the Code. 
 
Furthermore, Article 34 of the Family Code 
provides that no license shall be necessary 
for the marriage of a man and a woman who 
have lived together as husband and wife for 
at least five years and without any legal 
impediment to marry each other. The 
contracting parties shall state the foregoing 
facts in an affidavit before any person 
authorized by law to administer oaths. The 
solemnizing officer shall also state under 
oath that he ascertained the qualifications of 
the contracting parties and found no legal 
impediment to the marriage.  
 
In this case, it is apparent in the Certificate of 
Marriage between Sue Ann and Joseph that 
there is no marriage license. However, 
they marked the box with the statement “No 
marriage license was necessary. The 
marriage being solemnized under Art. 34 of 
Executive Order No. 209.” But no fact shows 
that the two executed an affidavit as required 
by the said provision. 
 
There is also no fact showing that Sue Ann 
and Joseph actually lived together as 
husband and wife for five years prior to 
the time of the celebration of their 
marriage (January 26, 1996). Records show 
that it was only in December 1995 when the 
two officially got together as sweethearts. 
Thus, the marriage between Sue Ann and 
Joseph is declared void due to the absence 
of a valid marriage license. (★Sue Ann 
Bounsit-Torralba v. Joseph B. Torralba,  
G.R. No. 214392, December 7, 2022, as 
penned by J. Gaerlan) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Exemption from license requirement 
 
a. Articulo mortis (Art. 27, 31 & 32)  
 
1. Either or both of the contracting parties 
are at the point of death.  
 
2. Marriage shall remain valid even if the 
ailing party subsequently survives.  
 
A witness should sign the dying party’s 
signature if the latter be physically unable to 
do so (Cruz vs.  Catandes, CA., 39 O.G. No. 
18, p. 324).  
 
b. Remote Places (Art. 28)  
 
The residence of either party is located 
where  there is no means of transportation 
to enable  such party to appear personally 
before the  local civil registrar.  
 
c. Cohabitation for at least 5 years (Art. 
34, FC)  
 
Requisites:  
 
1. The parties must have been living 
together  as husband and wife for at least 5 
years;  
 
 
 
2. The parties must have no legal 
impediment to marry each other;  
 
3. The fact of absence of legal impediment 
between the parties must be present at the 
time of marriage; and  
 


<!-- PAGE 32 -->

 
 
23 
4. The parties must execute an affidavit 
stating that they have lived together for at 
least five years and are without legal 
impediment to marry each other (Orja-
Manzano vs. Sanchez, A.M. No. MTJ-00-
1329, March 8, 2008).  
 
The five-year period must be:  
 
(1) COntinuous and uninterrupted; 
 
(2) Counted BAckwards from the day of the 
celebration of marriage; and 
 
(3) Characterized by EXclusivity, meaning, 
no third party was involved at any time 
within the five years (Niñal vs. Bayadog,  
G.R. No. 133778, March 14, 2000). 
 
d. Solemnized outside the Philippines 
where no marriage license is required by 
the country where it is solemnized 
 
If the marriage was celebrated before the 
diplomatic or consular officials of the 
Republic of the Philippines in a foreign 
country, marriage license is still required 
(Art.17, NCC). 
 
 
3) Marriage ceremony – which takes place 
with the: 
  
a. Personal appearance of the contracting 
parties before solemnizing officer; 
b. Their personal declaration that they take 
each other as husband and wife; and 
c.  In the presence of not less than two 
witnesses of legal age (Art. 3, FC).

d. Rights and Obligations Between 
Husband and Wife 
 
1. Essential marital obligations: 
 
a) Live together which includes consortium 
(cohabitation) and copulation (sexual 
intercourse); 
 
XPN: The Court may exempt a spouse 
from the obligation to live together for the 
following reasons: 
 
i. The other spouse should live abroad. 
 
ii. If there are other valid compelling 
reasons. 
 
NOTE: The exceptions would not apply if 
the same is not compatible with the 
solidarity of the family. (Art. 69(2), FC) 
b) Observe mutual love, respect, fidelity; 
and 
c) Render mutual help and support.  
 
2. Fix the family domicile; (Art. 69, FC) 
 
In case of disagreement the Court shall 
decide. The Court may also exempt one 
spouse from living with the other if the latter 
should live abroad or if there are other valid 
and compelling reasons for the exemption. 
However, such exemption shall not apply if 
the same is not compatible with the solidarity 
of the family. 
 
3. Jointly support the family; (Art. 70, FC) 
 
a. Expenses shall be paid from the 
community property; 
 
b. In the absence thereof from the income 
or fruits of their separate properties; and 
 
c. In the absence or insufficiency thereof 
from their separate properties. 
 
4. Manage the household; (Art. 72, FC) 
 
5. Not to neglect duties, or commit acts 
which tend to bring danger, dishonor, or 
injury to family; (Art. 72, FC) 
 
6. Either spouse may practice any 
legitimate 
profession/business, 
even 
without the consent of the other. 
 
The other spouse may object on valid, 
serious, and moral grounds. In case of 
disagreement, 
the 
court 
shall 
decide 
whether: 
 
a. Objection is proper; and 
 
b. Benefit has accrued to the family prior 
to the objection or thereafter. If the benefit 
accrued prior to the objection, the 
resulting obligation shall be enforced 
against the community property. If the 
benefit accrued thereafter, such obligation 
shall be enforced against the separate 
property of the spouse who has not 
obtained consent. (Art. 73, FC as 
amended by R.A. No. 10572) 
 
Other obligations of spouses 
 
1. Exercise the duties and enjoy the rights 
of parents; 
 
2. Answer for civil liability arising from injuries 
caused by children below 18; and 
 
3. Exercise parental authority over children’s 
property 
 
Essential marital obligations cannot be 
compelled by court

Performance 
of 
essential marital

Arroyo, G.R. No. L-17014, 11 Aug. 1921)

XPNs: 
 
1. Giving mutual help and support. 
 
A husband is liable to pay the expenses 
arising from the medical assistance

(Pelayo v. Lauron, G.R. No. L-4089, 12

2. Wife left the conjugal home for justifiable

and

to
