2. Validity of Marriages Celebrated 
Abroad 
 
Where a marriage between a Filipino citizen 
and a foreigner is validly celebrated and a 
divorce is thereafter validly obtained abroad 
by the alien spouse capacitating him or her 
to remarry, the Filipino spouse shall have 
capacity to remarry under Philippine law. 
(Art. 26, Family Code - As amended by 
Executive Order 227) 
 
The validity of marriages celebrated abroad 
is a complex matter governed by both 
Philippine law and international private law 
principles. Under Article 26 of the Family 
Code 
of 
the 
Philippines, 
marriages 
solemnized outside the Philippines are 
generally valid, provided they are valid under 
the laws of the country where they were 
celebrated. This reflects the principle of lex 
loci celebrationis, which upholds the validity 
of a marriage based on the law of the place 
of its celebration. 
 
Grounds Available to Have Marriages 
Celebrated Abroad Declared Null And 
Void In The Philippines 
 
Although a marriage celebrated abroad may 
be valid under the laws of the foreign 
jurisdiction, it may still be declared null and 
void in the Philippines if it falls under 
grounds established by Philippine law. The 
grounds for nullity are found in Articles 35, 
36, 37, and 38 of the Family Code, as well 
as jurisprudence interpreting these 
provisions. These include: 
 
1. Lack 
of 
Essential 
or 
Formal 
Requisites 
 
Under Article 2, a valid marriage must have 
essential and formal requisites, including 
legal capacity of the parties, consent freely 
given, authority of the solemnizing officer, a 
valid marriage license, and solemnization. If 
any of these requisites are absent or 
defective, the marriage is void ab initio. 
 
2. Psychological Incapacity 
 
A marriage may be declared void under 
Article 36 if one or both parties were 
psychologically incapacitated to comply with 
the 
essential 
marital 
obligations. 
This 
ground, elucidated in Santos v. CA (G.R. 
No. 112019, January 4, 1995) and Republic 
v. Molina (G.R. No. 108763, February 13, 
1997), applies even to marriages celebrated 
abroad. The psychological incapacity must 
be grave, pre-existing, and incurable. 
 
3. Incestuous Marriages 
 
Marriages 
between 
ascendants 
and 
descendants of any degree or between 
siblings, whether legitimate or illegitimate, 
are void under Article 37. 
 
4. Marriages Void for Reasons of 
Public Policy 
 
Under Article 38, marriages between 
relatives by collateral blood up to the fourth 
civil degree, step-parents and step-children, 


<!-- PAGE 35 -->

 
 
26 
parents-in-law 
and 
children-in-law, 
and 
others listed in the provision, are void. 
 
5. Bigamy 
 
A marriage celebrated abroad where one 
party is already validly married in the 
Philippines or elsewhere is void under 
Article 35(4). In Tenchavez v. Escaño 
(G.R. No. L-19671, November 29, 1965), the 
Supreme 
Court 
emphasized 
that 
the 
subsistence of a prior valid marriage renders 
subsequent marriages void. 
 
6. Non-Recognition 
of 
Foreign 
Divorce 
 
Under Article 26(1), a Filipino citizen cannot 
obtain a valid divorce abroad, as Philippine 
law does not recognize divorce for Filipinos. 
However, under Article 26(2), if a foreign 
spouse obtains a divorce validly abroad, the 
Filipino spouse may remarry, as clarified in 
Republic v. Manalo (G.R. No. 221029, April 
24, 2018). 
 
7. Fraud, Force, or Intimidation 
 
While these are grounds for annulment under 
Article 45, marriages procured through 
fraud, force, or intimidation may still be 
declared void if they render consent vitiated 
to the extent that no valid marriage was 
formed. 
 
Relevance of the Nationality of the Parties 
in Relation to the Available Grounds for 
Declaration of Nullity of Mixed Marriages 
in the Philippines 
 
The nationality of the parties plays a 
crucial role in determining the validity of 
mixed marriages and the grounds for their 
nullity or annulment under Philippine law. 
This principle arises from the interaction of 
domestic laws, international law, and the 
conflict of laws rules. 
 
 
 
 
1. Lex Nationali (Law of the Nationality) 
 
Under Philippine law, the personal status of 
an individual, including capacity to marry, is 
governed by the laws of their nationality. This 
is provided under Article 15 of the Civil 
Code, which states that laws relating to 
family rights and duties are binding upon 
Filipino citizens even if they reside or marry 
abroad. 
 
For example, a Filipino citizen marrying 
abroad remains subject to the essential 
requisites of marriage under Article 2 of the 
Family Code. If a Filipino marries a 
foreigner, any violation of these requisites 
(e.g., bigamy or lack of legal capacity) 
renders the marriage void, regardless of its 
validity under foreign law. 
 
2. Foreign Law Governing the Foreign 
Spouse 
 
In mixed marriages, the foreign spouse’s 
capacity to marry is governed by their 
national law. However, this does not 
preclude the application of Philippine law in 
determining the validity of the marriage when 
it is sought to be recognized or annulled in 
the Philippines. 
 
For instance, if a foreigner validly divorces 
their spouse under their national law, the 
Filipino spouse may remarry under Article 
26(2). 
 
Where a marriage between a Filipino citizen 
and a foreigner is validly celebrated and a 
divorce is thereafter validly obtained abroad 
by the alien spouse capacitating him or her 
to remarry, the Filipino spouse shall have 
capacity to remarry under Philippine law.  
 
3. Application of Public Policy 
 
Marriages that violate public policy, such as 
those listed under Articles 37 and 38, are 
void even if valid under the national law of the 
foreign spouse or the law of the place of 
celebration. 
This 
ensures 
that 
the 


<!-- PAGE 36 -->

 
 
27 
fundamental principles of Philippine family 
law are upheld. 
For example, a marriage between a Filipino 
and their step-child may be valid under the 
foreign spouse’s national law but remains 
void under Article 38(2) due to public policy. 
 
4. Recognition of Foreign Decrees 
 
For a foreign decree to be recognized in the 
Philippines, it must be proven valid and 
existing under the foreign law and must 
comply with procedural rules for recognition 
in Philippine courts, as established in Fujiki 
v. Marinay (G.R. No. 196049). 
 
Proof of the Foreign Law 
 
Philippine courts do not take judicial notice of 
foreign laws; they must be alleged and 
proven as facts during the proceedings. This 
is in accordance with Section 24, Rule 132 
of the Rules of Court, which requires 
evidence of foreign law to be presented 
through: 
 
 A copy of the foreign statute or ruling 
duly authenticated by the Philippine 
embassy or consulate. 
 Testimony of an expert on the foreign 
law, if applicable. 
 
Proof of Validity Under the Foreign Law 
 
The party seeking recognition of the foreign 
decree must establish that the decree is valid 
and enforceable in the jurisdiction where it 
was issued. This typically requires: 
 
 Certified copies of the decree or 
judgment. 
 Evidence 
of 
compliance 
with 
the 
procedural rules of the foreign court. 
 
3. Mixed Marriages and Foreign 
Divorce 
 
Requisites:  
 
a. There is a valid marriage that has been 
celebrated between a Filipino citizen and 
a foreigner; and  
 
b. A valid divorce is obtained abroad 
capacitating the alien spouse and the 
Filipino spouse to remarry (Art. 26(2), 
NCC).   
 
Requirements to prove foreign marriage:  
 
1. The existence of the pertinent provision of 
the foreign marriage law; and  
 
2. The celebration and performance of the 
marriage in accordance with the said law 
(Fujiki vs. Marinay G.R. No. 196049, June 
26, 2013; Corpuz vs. Sto. Tomas, G.R. No. 
186571, 11 August 2010; Garcia vs. Recio, 
G.R. No. 138322, October 2, 2001; Adong 
vs. Cheong Seng Gee, G.R. No. 18081, 
March 3, 1922).  
 
3. The party pleading it must prove the 
divorce as a fact and demonstrate its 
conformity to the foreign law allowing it.   
 
4. It must be shown that the divorce  decree 
allows the former spouse to  remarry as 
specifically required in Art.  26 of the 
Family Code. 
 
Effect of Foreign Divorce 
 
GR: Divorce contemplates the  dissolution of 
the lawful union for cause arising  after the 
marriage. Absolute divorce is not  recognized 
in the Philippines (Tolentino, Civil Code  of 
the Philippines, Vol. 1, 2004 ed.).  
 
XPN: Where a marriage between a Filipino  


<!-- PAGE 37 -->

 
 
28 
citizen and a foreigner is validly celebrated 
and a  divorce is thereafter validly obtained 
abroad by  the alien spouse capacitating him 
or her to  remarry, the Filipino spouse shall 
have capacity  to remarry under Philippine 
law (Art. 26 [2], FC). 
 
The purpose of Paragraph 2 of Article 26  of 
the Civil Code is to avoid the absurd  
situation where the Filipino spouse  remains 
married to the alien spouse  who, after a 
foreign divorce decree that  is effective in the 
country where it was  rendered, is no longer 
married to the  Filipino spouse.  
 
a. REPUBLIC V. MANALO, 831 PHIL. 33 
(2018) 
 
Facts: The case involves Maria Virginia 
Manalo, a Filipino citizen, who sought 
recognition of a foreign divorce obtained by 
her Japanese husband in Japan. The 
Japanese 
court 
granted 
the 
divorce, 
dissolving their marriage. Manalo filed a 
petition in the Philippines to have the foreign 
divorce decree recognized so she could 
remarry. The Office of the Solicitor General 
(OSG), representing the Republic of the 
Philippines, opposed the petition, arguing 
that Philippine law does not recognize 
divorce, except for Muslim Filipinos under 
special laws. 
 
Issue: Whether a Filipino citizen can invoke 
a foreign divorce decree obtained by a 
foreign spouse to terminate their marital ties 
under Philippine law. 
 
Ruling: The Supreme Court ruled in favor of 
Manalo, affirming that a foreign divorce 
obtained by a foreign spouse may be 
recognized under Philippine law. The key 
points of the decision are as follows: 
 
Application of Article 26(2) of the 
Family Code: 
 
Article 26(2) states that if a marriage is 
between a Filipino and a foreigner, and 
the foreign spouse obtains a valid divorce 
abroad that allows them to remarry, the 
Filipino spouse shall likewise have the 
capacity to remarry under Philippine law. 
 
The Court clarified that this provision 
applies even if the divorce is initiated and 
obtained solely by the foreign spouse, 
provided it effectively dissolves the marital 
bond. 
 
Recognition of Foreign Divorce 
 
The Court recognized the principle of comity 
of nations, acknowledging the validity of 
foreign 
divorce 
decrees 
obtained 
in 
compliance with the laws of the foreign 
spouse's nationality. 
 
However, the Filipino spouse must prove the 
validity of the foreign divorce through proper 
judicial recognition proceedings in Philippine 
courts, requiring evidence of the divorce law 
of the foreign country and proof of its 
application. 
 
 
4. Void Marriages 
 
Void Marriages are those which are 
inexistent from the very beginning. It is ipso 
facto void without need of any judicial 
declaration of nullity unless for purposes of 
remarriage. A judicial declaration of its nullity 
is required before a subsequent marriage 
can 
be 
contracted, 
otherwise, 
the 
subsequent marriage is also void and may be 
subjected to bigamy.  
 
A void marriage can be questioned even 
after  the death of either party (Niñal vs. 
Bayadog,  G.R. No. 133778, March 14, 
2000).  
 
The clause “on the basis solely of a final  
judgment declaring such marriage void” 
(Art.  40, FC) denotes that such final 


<!-- PAGE 38 -->

 
 
29 
judgment declaring the previous marriage 
void need not be obtained only for purposes 
of remarriage (Domingo vs. CA, G.R. No. 
104818, Sept. 17, 1993). 
  
 
a. DIFFERENT GROUNDS FOR NULLITY 
 
Mnemonic: 18 VOID Lice Bi-N-I  
 
 18 (below 18) 
 VOID (See item No. 6) 
 Lice (See item No. 3) 
 Bi (See item No. 4) 
 N (See item No. 2) 
 I (See item No. 5) 
 
 
 
1. Those contracted by any party Below 
eighteen years (18) of age even with the 
consent of parents or guardians (Art. 35 
(1),FC);  
 
 
2. Those solemnized by any person Not 
legally authorized to perform marriages; 
unless such marriages were contracted with 
either or both parties believing in good faith 
that the solemnizing officer had the legal 
authority to do so (Art. 35 (2), FC);  
 
 
3. Those solemnized without License, 
except those covered in Chapter 2 of the 
FC (Art. 35 (3), FC);  
 
Article 
35 
provides 
that 
marriages 
solemnized without a license shall be void 
from the beginning except for those stated in 
the Code. Furthermore, Article 34 of the 
Family Code provides that no license shall be 
necessary for the marriage of a man and a 
woman who have lived together as husband 
and wife for at least five years and without 
any legal impediment to marry each other. In 
this case, it is apparent in the Certificate of 
Marriage between Sue Ann and Joseph that 
there is no marriage license. There is also no 
fact showing that Sue Ann and Joseph 
actually lived together as husband and wife 
for five years prior to the time of the 
celebration of their marriage (January 26, 
1996).  (★Sue Ann Bounsit-Torralba v. 
Joseph B. Torralba, G.R. No. 214392, 
December 7, 2022, as penned by J. 
Gaerlan) 
 
 
4. Those Bigamous or polygamous 
marriages not failing under Article 41 (Art. 
35 (4), FC);  
 
Bigamous marriage refers to marriage 
contracted during the subsistence of a 
previous marriage.  
 
The subsequent marriage in Article 41 is 
perfectly valid when all three requisites 
were present before the celebration of the 
subsequent marriage:  
 
a. the prior spouse had been absent for 
four consecutive years or two years 
where there is danger of death under the 
circumstances stated in Art. 391 of the 
Civil Code;  
 
b. the spouse present had a well-founded 
belief that the absent spouse was 
already dead; and  
 
c. the spouse present obtained a judicial 
declaration of presumptive death  
 
 


<!-- PAGE 39 -->

 
 
30 
5. Those contracted through mistake of 
one of the contracting parties as to the 
Identity of the other (Art. 35 [5], FC);   
a. Not 
applicable 
to 
mistake 
of 
circumstances.  
b. The mistake in identity refers to the actual 
physical identity of the other contracting 
party (Rabuya, The Law on  Persons and 
Family Relations, 2006, p. 213).  
 
6. Those subsequent marriages that are 
VOID under Article 53  
 
In case one of the spouses, who intend to 
remarry, failed to register the judgment of 
annulment or declaration of absolute nullity 
of the marriage, the partition and distribution 
of the properties of the spouses, and the 
delivery of the children’s legitime, the 
subsequent marriage shall be void (Art. 53, 
FC). 
 
 
7. Psychological Incapacity (Article 36, 
Civil Code) 
 
A marriage contracted by any party who, at 
the 
time 
of 
the 
celebration, 
was 
psychologically incapacitated to comply with 
the essential marital obligations of marriage, 
shall likewise be VOID even if such 
incapacity becomes manifest only after its 
solemnization. (Article 36, Civil Code) 
 
Psychological incapacity is characterized 
by mutual incompatibility and antagonism 
between the spouses, arising from their 
respective personality structures. For mutual 
incompatibility and antagonism to constitute 
psychological incapacity, the same must be 
characterized by the elements of: 
(a) GRAVITY, i.e., it must be serious such 
that the spouses would be incapable of 
carrying out the ordinary duties required in a 
marriage; 
 
(b) JURIDICAL ANTECEDENCE, i.e., it 
must be rooted and shown in the history of 
the spouses antedating their marriage 
though the overt manifestations may emerge 
substantially and significantly only after the 
marriage; and 
 
c) INCURABILITY, i.e., it must not be 
susceptible to any cure, or even if it were 
otherwise, the cure would be beyond the 
means and inclination of the spouses.  
 
Note: Each of these elements must be 
proven clearly and convincingly. 
 
Relevant Rules/Jurisprudence 
 
Since psychological incapacity is no longer 
considered a mental incapacity nor a 
personality disorder, expert opinion is NO 
LONGER 
NECESSARY 
in 
proving 
psychological incapacity (Tan-Andal v. 
Andal).  


<!-- PAGE 40 -->

 
 
31 
 
 
It clarified, though, that there must be proof 
"of the durable or enduring aspects of a 
person's personality, called 'personality 
structure,' which manifests itself through 
clear 
acts 
of 
dysfunctionality 
that 
undermines the family.  
 
 
The spouse's personality structure must 
make it impossible for them to understand 
and, more important, to comply with their 
essential marital obligations." For this 
purpose, 
testimonies 
of 
ordinary 
witnesses may suffice to support a 
finding of psychological incapacity 
 
Such ordinary witnesses need only "to have 
been present in the life of the spouses 
before the latter contracted marriage" in 
order that they "may testify on behaviors 
that they have consistently observed 
from 
the 
supposedly 
incapacitated 
spouse." (Zeth D. Fopalan V. Neil F. 
Fopalan, GR NO. 250287 | 2022-07-20) 
 
In Tan-Andal v. Andal (G.R. No. 196359, 
May 11, 2021), the Supreme Court clarified 
that psychological incapacity need not be a 
mental disorder and must be proven by 
totality of evidence. Expert testimony is no 
longer mandatory but remains helpful. 
 
 
8. Incestuous Marriage (Article 37, Civil 
Code) 
 
Marriages 
between 
the 
following 
are 
incestuous and void from the beginning, 
whether relationship between the parties be 
legitimate or illegitimate: 
 
(1) Between ascendants and descendants of 
any degree; and 
(2) Between brothers and sisters, whether of 
the full or half blood.  
 
 
9. Void Marriages By Reasons Of Public 
Policy (Article 38, Civil Code) 
 
Marriages 
between 
the 
following 
are 
incestuous and void from the beginning, 
whether relationship between the parties be 
legitimate or illegitimate: 
 
(1) Between ascendants and descendants of 
any degree; and 
(2) Between brothers and sisters, whether of 
the full or half blood.  
 
 
10. Void Marriages By Reasons Of Public 
Policy (Article 38, Civil Code) 
 
(1) 
Between 
collateral 
blood 
relatives 
whether legitimate or illegitimate, up to the 
fourth civil degree; 
(2) Between step-parents and step-children; 
(3) Between parents-in-law and children-in-
law; 
(4) Between the adopting parent and the 
adopted child; 
(5) Between the surviving spouse of the 
adopting parent and the adopted child; 
(6) Between the surviving spouse of the 
adopted child and the adopter; 


<!-- PAGE 41 -->

 
 
32 
(7) Between an adopted child and a 
legitimate child of the adopter; 
(8) Between adopted children of the same 
adopter; and 
(9) Between parties where one, with the 
intention to marry the other, killed that 
other person's spouse, or his or her own 
spouse. 
 
 
11. Other void marriages 
 
1. Marriages in a play, drama, or movie;  
2. Marriages between two boys or two girls;  
3. Marriages in jest;  
4. Common law marriages;  
5. Marriages between persons of the same 
sex, even if celebrated abroad;   
6. Marriages where parties merely signed a 
marriage contract; and   
7. Marriages where the parties did not 
personally appear before the solemnizing 
authority (Paras, Persons and Family 
Relations Vol. 1 p. 441). 
 
 
b. LEGAL STANDING TO FILE A PETITION 
FOR DECLARATION OF NULLITY 
 
Legal standing refers to a party's right to 
bring an action. Under A.M. No. 02-11-10-
SC (Rule on Declaration of Absolute Nullity 
of Void Marriages and Annulment of 
Voidable Marriages), only the following have 
standing to file a petition for nullity of 
marriage: 
 
1. Either Spouse: A husband or wife 
directly affected by the marriage may file 
the petition. 
 
2. Guardian or Representative: When 
one spouse is incapacitated, the petition 
may be filed by a legal guardian or 
representative. 
 
3. Public Prosecutor or Fiscal: In specific 
cases involving public interest, such as 
child marriages prohibited by R.A. No. 
11596, the public prosecutor may initiate 
proceedings. 
 
 
c. COLLATERAL ATTACK TO ASSAIL 
VALIDITY OF A MARRIAGE 
 
A collateral attack occurs when the validity 
of a marriage is questioned indirectly in an 
unrelated 
proceeding. 
Philippine 
law 
generally prohibits collateral attacks on 
marriage validity: 
 
GR: Marriage Enjoys Presumption of 
Validity 
 
Marriages are presumed valid unless 
declared null in a direct action. 
 
XPN: In De Castro v. Assidao-De Castro 
(G.R. No. 160172), the Supreme Court 
reiterated that only a direct action may nullify 
a marriage, EXCEPT in criminal cases where 
bigamy is charged, which needs further proof 
of the subsistence of a prior valid marriage. 
 
 
d. 
LEGAL 
CONSEQUENCES 
OF 
DECLARING A MARRIAGE AS NULL AND 
VOID 
 
The declaration of nullity renders a marriage 
void ab initio, meaning it is as though the 
marriage never existed. This has specific 
legal 
consequences 
affecting 
property, 
children, remarriage, and succession. 
 Property Relations 
 
Article 147 and Article 148 of the Family 
Code govern the property relations of 
cohabiting parties in a void marriage: 
 
Article 147 applies to parties living together 
in good faith, believing they were validly 
married. The property acquired during their 


<!-- PAGE 42 -->

 
 
33 
cohabitation is considered co-owned in equal 
shares unless proven otherwise. 
Article 148 applies to parties who knowingly 
live together in bad faith or where one or both 
parties are in an existing valid marriage. In 
this case, only properties acquired through 
actual contributions are co-owned, and the 
share is proportional to their contribution. 
 Legitimacy of Children 
 
Article 54 of the Family Code protects 
children born of void marriages, provided at 
least one party entered into the marriage in 
good faith. These children are considered 
legitimate and enjoy full rights to inheritance 
and other benefits. 
 Right to Remarry 
 
Once a marriage is declared null and void, 
the parties regain the legal capacity to 
remarry. However, this right becomes 
effective only after the court’s judgment of 
nullity has attained finality. 
 
 Effect on Succession 
 
The declaration of nullity severs the 
inheritance rights between the spouses. In 
void marriages, neither spouse can inherit 
from the other, as there is no valid marital tie. 
 
Legitimate children, however, remain entitled 
to inherit from both parents under the rules of 
intestate succession. 
 
 
 
 
 
5. Voidable Marriages 
Voidable marriage is considered valid and 
produces all its civil effects, until it is set 
aside by final judgment of a competent court 
in an action for annulment (Suntay vs. 
Cojuangco-Suntay, G.R.  No. 183053, 
October 10, 2012) 
 
GR: Defects of a voidable marriage can be 
ratified by free cohabitation or prescription.   
 
XPNs: The following defects are not subject 
to ratification:  
 
(i) impotency 
which 
appears 
to 
be 
incurable; and 
(ii) STD that is found to be serious and 
incurable. These defects, however, may 
be subjected to prescription (Niñal vs. 
Bayadog, G.R. No. 133778, March 14, 
2000).  
GROUNDS 
 
1. Absence of parental consent (required 
when party is at least 18 but below 21 years 
of age under Art. 14, FC);  
 
Father, mother, surviving parent or guardian, 
or persons having legal charge of the party 
must give consent. However, if the child is 
illegitimate, consent of the mother is required 
since it is under the latter’s parental authority 
(Rabuya, The Law on Persons and Family 
Relations, 2006, p. 298).   
 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 43 -->

 
 
34 
2. Unsoundness of mind  
 
The sane spouse can file an action for 
annulment ONLY if he/she has no knowledge 
of the other spouse’s insanity at the time of 
marriage, otherwise, only the insane or the 
latter’s relatives may file an action for 
annulment (Rabuya, The Law on Persons 
and Family Relations, 2021, p. 301).  
 
The marriage may be ratified by the insane 
party, after coming to reason, freely 
cohabited with the other party (Art.  45[2], 
FC).  
 
The true test is whether the party concerned 
could intelligently consent; that is, he knew 
what contract he is entering into (Hoadley vs. 
Hoadley, 244 N.Y.  424).  
 
Intoxication, Somnambulism at the time of 
the wedding, and drug addiction which 
results in lack of capacity to give consent is 
equivalent to unsoundness of mind (McKnee 
vs. McKnee, 49 Nev. 90; See also 15 
Sanches Roman 528).  
 
A person is presumed to be of sound mind at 
any particular time and the condition is 
presumed to continue to exist, in the absence 
of proof to the contrary (Mendezona vs. 
Osamiz, G.R. No.  143370, February 6,2002; 
See also 29 Am Jur 2d Evidence § 295; 
Norwood v. Norwood, 207 Ga 148, 60 SE2d 
449).  
 
 
3. Fraud 
 
Non-disclosure or concealment of certain 
circumstances when materiality can affect 
the essence of marriage (Rabuya, The Law 
on Persons and Family Relations, 2006, p.  
303). 
Circumstances Constituting Fraud in 
Marriage (Art. 46, FC)  
 
 
 
a. 
non-disclosure 
of 
a 
previous 
conviction by final judgment of the other 
party of a crime involving moral turpitude. 
 
Moral turpitude is any act done contrary to 
justice, honesty, principle or good morals; or 
an act of baseness, vileness or depravity in 
private and social duties which a man owes 
to his fellowmen or to society in general, 
contrary to the accepted and customary rule 
of right and duty between man and man 
(Rabuya, The Law on Persons and Family 
Relations, 2006, p. 304).  
 
No other misrepresentation as to character, 
health, rank, fortune or chastity shall 
constitute such fraud as will give grounds for 
an action for the annulment of marriage (Art. 
46 [2], FC).  
 
Non-disclosure of a husband’s pre-marital 
relationship with another woman is not one of 
the enumerated circumstances that would 
constitute a ground for fraud (Anaya vs. 
Palaroan, G.R. No. L 27930, November 26, 
1970).  
 
b. Concealment by the wife of the fact that 
at the time of the marriage, she was 
pregnant by a man other than her 
husband.  
 
If the woman did not expressly inform the 
man of her pregnancy, but such physical 
condition was readily apparent to the man 
(seven months pregnant), he cannot claim 
lack of knowledge of such pregnancy (Buccat 
vs. Buccat, G.R. No. 47101, April 25, 1941).  
 
Even if the wife is pregnant with another  man 


<!-- PAGE 44 -->

 
 
35 
at the time of celebration of the  marriage, the 
marriage cannot be annulled  because the 
husband knew of such fact. In  the case, the 
present condition of the  woman was already 
evident at the time of  marriage (Ibid.) 
 
c. Concealment of sexually transmissible  
disease (STD), regardless of its nature,  
existing at the time of the marriage.  
 
d. Concealment of drug addiction, 
habitual alcoholism, or 
homosexuality/lesbianism 
 
Requisites for Annulment due to Drug 
Addiction:  
 
1. Existing at the time of the celebration 
of the marriage;  
2. Continuous;  
3. Appears to be incurable;  
4. Unknown to the other spouse at the 
time of marriage; and  
5. The case is filed within 5 years from 
discovery.  
Note: The enumeration in Art. 46, FC is 
exclusive.  
 
4. Consent was obtained by force, 
intimidation or undue influence;  
 
Force – must be one as to prevent the party 
from  acting as a free agent. His free will is 
destroyed  by fear or compulsion.  
 
Intimidation – one of the contracting parties 
is  compelled, by a reasonable and well-
grounded  fear of an imminent and grave evil 
upon the  person or property or upon those 
of his  spouse/descendant/ascendant to give 
consent.  
 
Undue influence – a person takes improper  
advantage of his power over the will of 
another,  depriving him of a reasonable 
freedom of choice.  
  
5. Impotence or impotentia copulandi  
 
It must be a continuing and incurable 
physical incapacity of consummating the 
marriage from the time of marriage up to the 
time of filing a petition for annulment. This 
refers to the physical inability to perform the 
act of sexual intercourse.   
 
The action must be brought by the potent 
spouse, and such spouse must be unaware 
of the other’s impotency (Keezer on Marriage 
and Divorce,  1946 Ed.,478).  
a) If both parties are impotent, the marriage 
cannot be annulled because no one is 
aggrieved (Sarao vs. Guevara [CA], 40 
O.G. [1st Sup] 263). 
b) The test is not the capacity to reproduce, 
but the capacity to copulate (Menciano 
vs. San Jose, G.R. No. L-1967, May 28, 
1951).  
The advance age of a person did not mean 
that sexual intercourse is no longer possible  
(People vs. Ablog, G.R. No. 125005, June 
28,  1999) 
 
 
Doctrine of Triennial Cohabitation   
 
If the wife still remains a virgin after living 
together with her husband for three years, 
the presumption is that the husband is 
impotent, and he will have to overcome this 
presumption (Tompkins vs. Tompkins, 92 
N.J. Eq. 113, 11 Atl. 599).  
 
Relative 
Impotence 
as 
Ground 
for 
Annulment  
 
When a man may not be able to have penile 
erection with his wife but can have it with 
another woman (Tompkins vs. Tompkins, 
supra.).  
 
Refusal to be examined by the husband or 
wife 
 
Refusal on the part of the man to submit 


<!-- PAGE 45 -->

 
 
36 
himself to physical examination raises the 
presumption of impotence (Keezer on 
Marriage and Divorce, 1946 Ed., pp. 480-
481). However, refusal by the wife does not 
raise such presumption because of the 
natural modesty of our native girls (Jimenez 
vs.  Cañizares, G.R. No. L-12790, August 31, 
1960). 
 
Sexually Transmitted Disease (STD), if 
incurable: 
  
Requisites:   
 
1. It must have existed at the time of the 
celebration of the marriage  
2. It is serious and appears to be incurable  
3. Unknown to both parties at the time of the 
marriage  
 
STD (Article 45 v. Article 46) 
ARTICLE 45 
ARTICLE 46 
Ground for annulment  The STD is a type of 
fraud which is a ground 
for annulment 
Does not have to be 
concealed  
Must be concealed 
Must be serious and 
incurable  
Need not be serious 
nor incurable 
The STD itself is the 
ground for annulment  
It is the concealment 
that gives rise to the 
annulment 
 
DEFENSES 
 
Procedural Defense/s 
 
1. Prescription 
 
a) For marriages solemnized without the 
required parental consent, the petition 
must be filed within five years after the 
party reaches the age of 21. This is 
because, at 21 years old, the party is 
presumed to have attained full legal 
capacity to consent to the marriage 
independently. 
b) For cases involving insanity, the petition 
may be filed at any time before the 
death of either party, acknowledging 
the lifelong vulnerability of the afflicted 
spouse or their guardians. However, the 
petition may also be filed by the insane 
spouse during a lucid interval or after 
regaining sanity. 
c) For fraud, force, intimidation, or undue 
influence, the petition must be filed 
within five years from the time of 
discovery of the fraud or from the 
cessation of the force, intimidation, 
or undue influence. This ensures that 
claims 
are 
made 
promptly 
while 
evidence remains available. 
d) For physical incapacity or incurable 
sexually 
transmitted 
diseases, 
the 
petition must be filed within five years 
from the date of the marriage, as these 
grounds pertain to defects that exist at 
the time of the marriage ceremony itself. 
 
Failure to file the petition within these 
timeframes constitutes a bar to the action, 
effectively preserving the validity of the 
marriage despite the existence of the alleged 
ground for annulment. 
 
2. Procedural Irregularities 
 
Under the Rules of Court, procedural 
defenses may be raised if there are defects 
in the filing or prosecution of the annulment 
case. 
For 
instance, 
failure 
to 
join 
indispensable parties, such as parents or 
guardians in cases of lack of parental 
consent, can render the action dismissible.  
 
Substantive Defense/s 
 
1. Cohabitation 
 


<!-- PAGE 46 -->

 
 
37 
a) Article 45 of the Family Code provides 
that certain grounds for annulment may 
be rendered inapplicable if the aggrieved 
party voluntarily cohabited with the 
other spouse after the ground ceased to 
exist. This defense is based on the 
principle of waiver. 
b) In cases of fraud, if the injured spouse 
freely cohabits with the other party after 
discovering the fraudulent act, the law 
considers this as a waiver of the right to 
annul the marriage. 
c) In cases of force, intimidation, or 
undue 
influence, 
the 
voluntary 
resumption of cohabitation after the 
coercive circumstances have ceased 
likewise extinguishes the ground for 
annulment. 
d) In cases of unsound mind, if the party 
regains sanity and then cohabits freely 
with the other spouse, the defect is 
deemed cured. 
 
2. Absence of Evidence 
 
The presumption of the validity of marriage is 
a cornerstone of Philippine family law. Under 
Article 220 of the Civil Code, all marriages 
are presumed valid unless proven otherwise. 
This presumption imposes a burden of 
proof on the petitioner seeking annulment. 
The Supreme Court, in the case of Montañez 
v. Cipriano (G.R. No. 231518, July 2, 2018), 
clarified that clear and convincing evidence 
is required to establish the ground for 
annulment. A mere allegation or conjecture 
is insufficient to overcome this strong 
presumption. 
 
In defending against an annulment petition, 
the respondent may argue that the petitioner 
has failed to present evidence that meets this 
evidentiary threshold. For example, if the 
ground alleged is fraud, the petitioner must 
present clear evidence of the fraudulent act 
and its causal connection to the consent 
given during the marriage. Without such 
proof, the petition must fail. 
 
LEGAL 
CONSEQUENCES 
OF 
ANNULMENT OF MARRIAGE 
 
The following legal consequences arise upon 
annulment: 
 
1) Status of the Children 
 
Under Article 54 of the Family Code, 
children conceived or born before the 
judgment of annulment becomes final and 
executory are considered legitimate. This 
ensures that the legitimacy and rights of the 
children, such as inheritance rights, are 
protected despite the dissolution of the 
marital union. 
 
2) Dissolution of Property Regime 
 
Upon annulment, the property regime of the 
spouses is dissolved and liquidated. This 
process follows the provisions of Articles 50 
and 51 of the Family Code. Specific 
consequences include: 
 
 The absolute community or conjugal 
partnership is terminated, and assets 
are liquidated. 
 If one spouse acted in bad faith, their 
share of the net profits of the property 
regime is forfeited. Forfeiture benefits 
the common children, children of the 
innocent 
spouse 
from 
a 
previous 
marriage, or, in their absence, the 
innocent spouse. 
 
3) Effects on Donations and Insurance 
Designations 
 
 Donations by reason of marriage remain 
valid unless the donee-spouse acted in 
bad faith. If bad faith is established, the 
donations made in favor of the guilty 
spouse are revoked automatically under 
Articles 43(3) and 44 of the Family 
Code. 


<!-- PAGE 47 -->

 
 
38 
 The innocent spouse may revoke the 
designation of the guilty spouse as a 
beneficiary in any insurance policy, even 
if the designation was stipulated as 
irrevocable (Article 43[4]). 
4) Successional Rights 
 
A spouse who acted in bad faith is 
disqualified from inheriting from the innocent 
spouse, either through testate or intestate 
succession, 
under 
Article 
43(5). 
This 
disqualification underscores the principle 
that bad faith in marriage nullifies certain 
benefits conferred by the marital relationship. 
 
5) Partition and Delivery of Presumptive 
Legitimes 
 
The presumptive legitimes of all common 
children are computed and delivered upon 
annulment as mandated by Article 51. The 
delivery of these legitimes in cash, property, 
or sound securities ensures that the 
children’s 
inheritance 
rights 
are 
not 
prejudiced. However, any property or value 
received under the annulment decree is 
considered an advance on their eventual 
legitime upon the death of either or both 
parents. 
 
6) Requirement of Registration 
 
Under Article 52, the judgment of annulment, 
along with the liquidation, partition, and 
delivery of presumptive legitimes, must be 
recorded in the civil registry and appropriate 
registries of property. Non-compliance with 
this requirement renders the judgment 
ineffective against third parties, emphasizing 
the importance of ensuring public notice and 
protecting the rights of creditors and third 
parties. 
 
7) Right to Remarry 
 
Either spouse may remarry after the final 
judgment 
of 
annulment, 
provided 
the 
requirements of Article 53—compliance with 
registration and liquidation—are met. Failure 
to comply with these requirements renders 
any subsequent marriage void. 
 
 
6. Legal Separation 
 
Legal separation is merely the separation of 
spouses from bed and board. While it permits 
the partial suspension of marital relations, 
the marriage bond still exists as the marital 
bonds are not severed as in the case of 
annulment or petition for nullity. 
 
a. GROUNDS 
 
 
1) Repeated physical violence or grossly 
abusive conduct directed against the 
petitioner, a common child, or a child of 
the petitioner;   
 
a. If the physical violence is directed against 
the wife, a common child or a child of the 
wife, the same is also punishable under 
R.A. 9262, otherwise known as the "Anti-
Violence Against Women and Their 
Children Act of 2004."   
 
b. Violence need not be physical; it is 
submitted that psychological and sexual 
violence and repeated verbal abuse may 
qualify as grossly abusive conduct 
(Rabuya, The Law on Persons and Family 
Relations, 2006, p. 352).   
 
c. "Psychological violence" refers to acts or 
omissions causing or likely to cause 
mental or emotional suffering of the victim 
such as but not limited to intimidation, 
harassment, 
stalking, 
damage 
to 


<!-- PAGE 48 -->

 
 
39 
property, public ridicule or humiliation, 
repeated verbal abuse, and mental 
infidelity. It includes causing or allowing 
the victim to witness the physical, sexual 
or psychological abuse of a member of the 
family to which the victim belongs, or to 
witness pornography in any form or to 
witness abusive injury to pets or to 
unlawful or unwanted deprivation of the 
right to custody and/or visitation of 
common children (Sec. 3 [c], R.A. No. 
9262). 
 
2) Physical violence or moral pressure to  
compel the petitioner to change religious  
or political affiliation;  
 
Moral pressure must be directed against  the 
petitioner-spouse. If the same is  directed on 
a common child or child of  the petitioner- 
spouse to compel the said  child to change 
religious or political  affiliation, there is no 
ground for legal  separation (Rabuya).  
 
3. Attempt of respondent to corrupt or  
induce the petitioner, a common child,  or 
a child of the petitioner, to engage in  
prostitution, or connivance in such  
corruption or inducement; It pertains to  a 
common child or the petitioner’s child,  
and not the respondent‘s child.  
 
4. 
Final 
judgment 
sentencing 
the  
respondent to imprisonment of more  
than six years, even if pardoned;  
 
Conviction must occur after the  celebration 
of the marriage. If  respondent is convicted in 
a final  judgment prior thereto, it is a ground 
for  annulment if the crime involves moral  
turpitude and the fact of conviction is  not 
disclosed to the other party (Rabuya,  The 
Law on Persons and Family Relations,  2006, 
p. 353). 
 
5.Drug addiction or habitual alcoholism of  
the respondent;  
 
Requisites:   
 
a. There should be no condonation or  
consent to the drug addiction;   
b. The action must be filed within 5  years 
from the occurrence of the  cause;  
c. Drug addiction arises during the  marriage 
and not at the time of  marriage.  
 
If such is present at the time of  marriage and 
was concealed  from the other party, it shall  
constitute as a ground for  annulment (Art.46 
[4], FC).  
 
6.Lesbianism or Homosexuality of the  
respondent;  
 
7. Contracting by the respondent of a  
subsequent bigamous marriage,  whether 
in the Philippines or abroad  
 
The subsequent marriage is void ab  initio but 
it does not affect the validity  of the prior 
marriage. Hence, the  remedy of the 
aggrieved party in the  prior marriage is legal 
separation  (Rabuya, The Law on Persons 
and Family  Relations, 2006, 354).  
 
8. Sexual infidelity or perversion  
 
Sexual infidelity need not be in the  form of 
adultery or concubinage  (Rabuya, The Law 
on Persons and Family  Relations, 2004, p. 
355).  
 
Sexual perversion includes all  unusual or 
abnormal sexual practices  which may be 
offensive to the feelings  or sense of decency 
of either the  husband or the wife (Tolentino, 
Persons  and Family Relations, 1990, p. 
323).  
 
9. Attempt by the respondent against the  
life of the petitioner;   
 
There must be an intent to kill and must not 
be justified or excused, as in the case of 
legitimate self-defense. 
 
10. Abandonment of petitioner without  
justifiable cause for more than one year.  
 
Note: These grounds for legal separation are 
exclusive.  


<!-- PAGE 49 -->

 
 
40 
 
Proof needed: Mere preponderance of 
evidence, will suffice to prove the existence 
of any of the grounds, although in Final 
Judgment sentencing the respondent to 
imprisonment of more  than 6 years, even if 
pardoned, previous  conviction is essential, 
in view of the  necessity of ―Final Judgment 
(Paras,  Civil Code of the Philippines 
Annotated Vol.  1, 2008, p. 543).  
 
 
b. DEFENSES 
 
1. Condonation 
 
Under Article 56(1), the petition is barred if 
the petitioner condoned the offense. This 
typically occurs when the innocent spouse 
resumes cohabitation with the offending 
spouse despite knowledge of the ground. 
2. Consent 
If the petitioner consented to the 
commission of the act, as provided under 
Article 56(2), the petition cannot prosper. 
For example, a spouse who allowed or 
encouraged the respondent’s infidelity 
cannot later file for legal separation based 
on such ground. 
3. Connivance 
Connivance refers to the petitioner’s active 
participation or inducement of the 
respondent’s commission of the act. Under 
Article 56(3), connivance bars the petition. 
4. Mutual Guilt 
 
When both parties are guilty of acts 
constituting grounds for legal separation, the 
petition is barred under Article 56(4). This is 
in line with the principle of clean hands, 
which prohibits a party at fault from seeking 
judicial relief. 
5. Prescription 
 
The action for legal separation must be filed 
within five years from the occurrence of 
the cause. Failure to file within this period 
results in the dismissal of the petition, as 
mandated by Article 57 of the Family Code. 
6. Collusion 
In compliance with Article 48, the court must 
ensure there is no collusion between the 
parties. If collusion is proven, the case is 
dismissed to preserve the integrity of the 
judicial process. 
 
 
c. LEGAL CONSEQUENCES OF LEGAL 
SEPARATION 
 
1. Separation of Property 
 
The 
absolute 
community 
or 
conjugal 
partnership is dissolved and liquidated. The 
net profits from the property regime are 
divided equally, unless one spouse is found 
to have acted in bad faith. In such cases, the 
guilty spouse forfeits their share of the profits 
in favor of the common children, the innocent 
spouse’s children from a prior marriage, or, 
in their absence, the innocent spouse. 
 
2. Custody of Children 
Custody of minor children is awarded to the 
innocent spouse, except when the court 
determines otherwise based on the best 
interests of the children. This provision aligns 
with Article 213 of the Family Code, which 
prioritizes the welfare of the child. 
 
3. Forfeiture of Rights to Inheritance 
 
The offending spouse loses the right to 
inherit from the innocent spouse under 
Articles 63(4) and 920 of the Civil Code, 


<!-- PAGE 50 -->

 
 
41 
whether 
through 
testate 
or 
intestate 
succession. 
 
4. Use of Surname 
 
The innocent spouse may opt to revert to 
their maiden name, as stipulated in Article 
370 of the Civil Code, particularly if 
continuing to use the surname of the 
offending spouse causes embarrassment or 
personal discomfort. 
 
5. Disqualification from Donations 
 
Donations between spouses made during 
the marriage are revoked by operation of law 
under Articles 43(3) and 44 of the Family 
Code, particularly if the offending spouse 
acted in bad faith. 
 
6. No Right to Remarry 
 
Unlike annulment or declaration of nullity, 
legal separation does not dissolve the marital 
bond, and thus neither party may remarry. 
 
 
d. EFFECTS OF RECONCILIATION 
 
1. On their personal relations – resumption 
of cohabitation and marital  relations;   
 
2. On proceedings for the decree of legal  
separation:  
 
a. The legal separation proceedings, if  still 
pending, shall thereby be  terminated at 
whatever stage; and   
 
b. The final decree of legal separation  shall 
be set aside, but the separation of  
property and any forfeiture of the  share of 
the guilty spouse already  effected shall 
subsist, unless the  spouses agree to 
revive their former  property regime (Art. 
66, FC).  
 
c. The court's order containing the  foregoing 
shall be recorded in the  proper civil 
registries.  
 
Without the decree of reconciliation, even if 
they reconcile the effects of the final decree 
of legal separation will remain (Rabuya).  
 
Agreement to revive former property  
regime upon reconciliation (Art. 67, FC)  
 
Agreement of revival and motion for its  
approval shall be filed in court in the same  
proceeding for legal separation and shall be  
executed under oath, where it shall specify:  
 
a. Properties to be contributed anew to the  
restored regime;   
b. Those to be retained as separated  
properties of each spouse; and   
c. Names of all their known creditors, their  
addresses and the amounts owing to  
each.  
 
The agreement of revival and the motion for 
its  approval shall be filed with the court in the 
same  proceeding for legal separation, with 
copies of  both furnished to the creditors 
named therein.  After due hearing, the court 
shall, in its order,  take measure to protect 
the interest of creditors  and such order shall 
be recorded in the proper  registries of 
properties.   
 
The recording of the ordering in the registries 
of  property shall not prejudice any creditor 
not  listed or not notified, unless the debtor-
spouse  has sufficient separate properties to 
satisfy the  creditor's claim.  
 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 51 -->

 
 
42 
7. Property Relations between the 
Spouses 
 
General Provisions 
 
The property relationship between husband 
and wife shall be governed in the following 
order:  
 
1. By marriage settlements executed before 
the marriage;  
2. By the provisions of the Civil Code; and  
3. By the local custom (Art. 74, FC). 
 
a. ANTE-NUPTIAL AGREEMENTS OR 
MARRIAGE SETTLEMENTS 
 
A 
marriage 
settlement 
is 
a 
written 
agreement executed between two parties 
who plan to get married, prior to the 
celebration of their marriage. This agreement 
fixes the terms and conditions that will 
govern their present and future property 
relations 
during 
the 
marriage. 
These 
agreements are more commonly known as 
“Ante-Nuptial Agreements.” 
 
The future spouses may, in the marriage 
settlements, agree upon any of the following: 
 
1. Regime of absolute Community; 
2. Conjugal partnership of gains; 
3. Complete separation of property; or 
4. Any other regime. 
 
In the ABSENCE of a marriage settlement, 
or when the regime agreed upon is VOID, the 
system of absolute community of property as 
established in this Code shall govern. 
 
Requisites of a Valid Marriage Settlement 
 
1) In Writing (Art. 77, FC) 
An agreement in consideration of marriage 
must be in writing; otherwise, it shall be 
unenforceable (Art. 1403[2C], NCC). 
 
Note: If the marriage is terminated by the 
death of one of the spouses and the surviving 
spouse 
remarries 
without 
settling 
the 
properties of the previous marriage within 
one (1) year from the deceased spouse's 
death, the mandatory regime of complete 
separation of property shall govern the 
subsequent marriage (Arts. 103 and 130, 
Family Code). 
 
2) Signed by the Parties (Art. 77, FC). 
 
3) Must not Contain Provisions Contrary to 
Law, Good Morals, Customs, Public 
Order, or Public Policy 
 
It must not be prejudicial to third persons 
(Art. 77, FC). 
 
4) Executed Before the Celebration of the 
Marriage (Art. 76, FC). 
 
5) Fixing the Terms and Conditions of their 
Property Relation. 
 
6) Registration (to Bind Third Persons) (Art. 
77, FC). 
 
7) Additional Signatories: 
 
a. A minor who may lawfully contract 
marriage may also execute a marriage 
settlement. However, it shall be valid 
only if the persons designated in Article 
14 (parents or guardians) to give 
consent to the marriage are made 
parties to the agreement (Art. 78, FC). 
 
b. If the party executing the settlement is 
under civil interdiction or any other 
disability, the guardian appointed by the 
court must be made a party to the 
settlement (Art. 79, FC). 
 


<!-- PAGE 52 -->

 
 
43 
Effect of marriage settlement if marriage  
does not take place  
 
GR: Everything stipulated in the settlements 
or contracts in consideration of a  future 
marriage, including donations between  the 
prospective spouses made therein, shall be  
rendered void if the marriage does not take 
place  (Art. 81, FC).  
 
XPN: Stipulations that do not depend upon 
the celebration of the marriages shall be valid 
(Art. 81, FC).  
 
Applicable Law for Marriage Settlements 
 
GR: In the absence of a contrary stipulation 
in a marriage settlement, the property 
relations of the spouses shall be governed 
by Philippine laws, regardless of the place 
of the celebration of the marriage and their 
residence (Art. 80, FC). 
 
XPNs: 
 
a. Both spouses are aliens; 
b. With respect to the extrinsic validity of 
contracts affecting property: 
 
1. Not situated in the Philippines and 
executed in the country where the 
property is located; and 
2. Situated in a foreign country whose 
laws require different formalities for 
its extrinsic validity (Art. 80, FC). 
 
 
b. DONATION PROPTER NUPTIAS 
 
Requisites of Donation Propter Nuptias 
 
1. It is made before the celebration of 
marriage. 
2. It must be made in consideration 
of the marriage. 
3. It must be made in favor of one or 
both of the future spouses. 
 
Limitation on Donations by Reason of 
Marriage 
 
If the regime agreed upon is other than 
absolute community, they cannot donate to 
each other in their marriage settlements 
more than (1/5) one-fifth of their present 
property. Any excess shall be considered 
VOID (Art. 84, FC). 
 
The donation may comprehend all the 
present property of the donor, or part thereof, 
provided he reserves, in full ownership or in 
usufruct, sufficient means for the support of 
himself, and all relatives who, at the time of 
the acceptance of the donation, are by law 
entitled to be supported by the donor. 
Without such reservation, the donation shall 
be reduced on petition of any person affected 
(Art. 750, NCC). 
 
No person may give or receive, by way of 
donation, more than he may give or receive 
by will. The donation shall be inofficious in all 
that it may exceed this limitation (Art. 752, 
NCC). 
 
Void donations 
 
GR: Every donation or grant of gratuitous 
advantage, direct or indirect, between the 
spouses during the marriage shall be VOID 
(Art. 87, FC). 
 
Reason: 
 
1. To prevent the weaker spouse from 
being influenced by the stronger 
spouse; 
2. To protect creditors; 
3. To prevent an indirect modification of 
the marriage settlement during marriage 
(Art. 76). 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 53 -->

 
 
44 
XPNs: 
 
1. Moderate gifts given on occasion of 
family rejoicing; or 
2. Donations mortis causa. 
 
Note: 
Donation 
between 
common-law 
spouses is void (Art. 87, FC). 
 
Void donations by the Spouses 
 
1. Those made between persons who were 
guilty of adultery or concubinage at the 
time of the donation; 
In this case, the action for declaration of 
nullity may be brought by the spouse of 
the donor or donee; and the guilt of the 
donor and donee may be proved by 
preponderance of evidence in the same 
action (Art. 739, NCC, last par.) 
2. Those made between persons found 
guilty of the same criminal offense, in 
consideration thereof; and 
3. Those made to a public officer or his wife, 
descendants and ascendants, by reason 
of his office (Art. 739, NCC) 
4. reason of his office (Art. 739, NCC) 
between the spouses during the marriage 
shall be void (Art. 87, FC). 
 
 
c. DIFFERENT PROPERTY REGIMES 
 
i. Absolute Community of Property (ACP) 
ii. Conjugal Partnership of Gains (CPG) 
iii. Complete Separation of Property (CSP) 
or Judicial Separation of Property (JSP) 
 
Commencement of Property Regime  
 
A property regime commences at the 
precise moment of the celebration of the 
marriage (i.e.  actual time the marriage is 
celebrated on a certain date).  
 
Any stipulation, express or implied, for the 
commencement of the community regime at 
any other time shall be VOID (Art. 88, FC).  
 
 
ABSOLUTE COMMUNITY OF PROPERTY 
REGIME 
 
ACP is a marriage settlement where spouses 
are  considered co-owners of ALL property 
brought  into the marriage which are not 
otherwise  excluded from the community 
property either by  the provisions of the 
Family Code or by the  marriage settlement 
(Rabuya, The Law on Persons  and Family 
Relations, 2006, p. 421).  
 
It applies when:  
 
1) Agreed upon in the marriage settlement; 
2) Do not choose any economic system of  
property regime and the marriage took 
place  during the effectivity of the Family 
Code  (on August 3, 1988 or after); or  
3) When marriage settlement is void (Art.75, 
FC). Commencement  
 
What constitutes community property 
 
GR: Community property shall consist of 
ALL property owned by the spouses at the 
time of the marriage or acquired thereafter 
(Art. 91, FC). 
 
XPNs: 
 
1. Property acquired during the marriage 
by gratuitous title by either spouse, its 
fruits and income, unless it is expressly 
otherwise 
provided 
by 
the 
donor, 
testator or grantor. 
2. For personal and exclusive use, except 
jewelry. 
3. Acquired before the marriage by either 
spouse who has legitimate descendants 
by a former marriage including the fruits 
and income (Art. 92, FC). 
4. Those excluded from the absolute 
community as in the marriage settlement 
(Art. 91, FC). 
 


<!-- PAGE 54 -->

 
 
45 
Presumption of inclusion in the absolute 
community 
 
Property acquired during the marriage is 
presumed to belong to the community, 
unless proven otherwise by strong and 
convincing evidence (Art. 93, FC). 
 
Liability of the Absolute Community of 
Property 
 
1. The support of the spouses, common 
children, and legitimate children of either 
spouse; 
2. Illegitimate children shall be governed by 
the provisions of the Code on Support; 
3. All debts and obligations contracted 
during marriage; 
4. Debts and obligations contracted by 
either spouse without the consent of the 
other; 
5. All taxes, liens, charges and expenses, 
including major or minor repairs; 
6. All taxes and expenses for mere 
preservation made during marriage; 
7. Expenses of either spouse to commence 
or complete a professional or vocational 
course; 
8. Antenuptial debt; 
9. The value of what is donated or promised 
by both spouses; Advances of the 
debtor-spouse upon liquidation of the 
community; and 
10. 
Expenses of litigation between the 
spouses unless the suit is found to be 
groundless. 
 
When the community property is insufficient 
to cover the foregoing liabilities, except par. 
9, the spouses shall be solidarily liable for the 
unpaid balance with their separate properties 
(Art. 94, FC). 
 
Procedure on Liquidation of the Absolute  
Community Assets and Liabilities  
 
 
1. An inventory of all the ACP properties and  
exclusive properties of each spouse;   
 
2. Payment of debts and obligations of 
the  absolute community;  
 
In case of insufficiency of said assets, the  
spouses shall be solidarily liable for the 
unpaid  balance with their separate 
properties taking  into consideration the 
charges and obligations  upon the ACP 
(Article 94, FC).  
 
3. Delivery of exclusive properties to 
spouses 
 
Whatever remains of the exclusive 
properties  of the spouses shall thereafter 
be delivered to  each of them.  
 
4. Net remainder = Net assets of ACP   
 
GR: The net remainder of the  properties of 
the absolute community which shall  be 
divided equally between husband and wife.   
 
XPN: 
  
a. Unless a different proportion or division  
was agreed upon in the marriage  
settlements or;   
b. There has been a voluntary waiver of 
such  share provided in this Code or;  
c. The share of the guilty spouse is forfeited. 
 
For purpose of computing the net profits  
subject to forfeiture in accordance with  
Articles 43, No. 2 (i.e., one of the spouses  
contracted subsequent marriage in bad  faith, 
his share shall be forfeited in favor of  the 
common children, if none, the children  of the 
guilty spouse by a previous marriage  or in 
default of children, the innocent  spouse) and 
63, No. 2 (i.e., the offending  spouse in case 
of legal separation shall have  no right to any 
share of the net profits  earned by the ACP) 
the said profits shall be  the increase in value 
between the market  value of the community 
property at the time  of the celebration of the 
marriage and the  market value at the time of 
its dissolution (Art. 102, FC).  


<!-- PAGE 55 -->

 
 
46 
5. Delivery of presumptive legitimes of 
the  common children.  
 
6. Adjudication of conjugal dwelling and 
lot.  
 
a. In accordance with agreement of the  
parties, if any;  
b. If no agreement, it shall be adjudicated  
to the spouse with whom the majority of  
the common children choose to remain; 
 
Dissolution 
of 
Absolute 
Community 
Regime 
 
 
Art. 99, FC. The absolute community 
terminates: 
 
1. Upon the death of either spouse; 
Effect of Termination of ACP due to 
death of one of the Spouses 
 
a. The community property shall be 
liquidated within one year from death of 
the deceased spouse in the same 
proceeding for the settlement of the estate 
of the deceased (Art. 103, FC). 
 
b. If no judicial settlement proceeding is 
instituted, the surviving spouse shall 
liquidate the community property either 
judicially or extrajudicially. 
 
2. When there is a decree of legal 
separation; 
 
3. When the marriage is annulled or 
declared void; or 
 
4. In case of judicial separation of 
property on the ground of: 
a. Civil interdiction;  
b. Declared absentee;  
c. Loss of parental authority; 
d. Abandonment and failure to comply 
with the obligations to the family;  
e. Abuse of power of administration;  
f. At the time of the petition, spouses are 
separated in fact for at least 1 year and 
the possibility for reconciliation is 
highly improbable (Art. 135, FC). 
 
 
CONJUGAL PARTNERSHIP OF GAINS 
REGIME 
 
In this regime, the spouses retain ownership 
of their separate property. However, the 
spouses place in a COMMON FUND the 
proceeds, Products, Income, and Fruits (PIF) 
of their separate property and those acquired 
by either or both spouses through their 
efforts or by chance.  
 
Commencement of CPG   
 
CPG shall commence at the precise moment 
the marriage is celebrated (Art. 107, FC in 
relation to Art. 88, FC).  
 
Presumption of CPG 
 
All property acquired during the marriage, 
whether the acquisition appears to have 
been made, contracted or registered in the 
name of one or both spouses, is presumed to 
be conjugal unless the contrary is proved 
(Art. 116, FC). 
 
Rules that govern the CPG  
 
The rules on the contract of partnership in all 
that  is not in conflict with what is expressly  
determined in the FC or by the spouses in 
their  marriage settlements (Art. 108, FC). 
 


<!-- PAGE 56 -->

 
 
47 
Note: There are 3 distinct patrimonies in this 
system, the husband’s capital property, the 
wife’s paraphernal property, and the conjugal 
property. 
 
What constitutes CPG - Those acquired 
by the spouses: 
 
1. By onerous title during marriage at the 
expense of the common fund; 
2. Through labor, work, profession, or 
industry of either or both spouses; 
3. All fruits, natural, industrial, or civil, due 
or received during marriage from common 
property, as well as net fruits from the 
exclusive property of each spouse; 
4. Share of either spouse in hidden 
treasure as finder or owner of property 
where treasure is found; 
5. Through occupation; 
6. Livestock existing upon dissolution of 
conjugal partnership of gains in excess of 
the number of each kind brought to the 
marriage by either spouse; and 
7. Those acquired by chance (Art. 117, FC). 
But losses from a game of chance shall be 
borne exclusively by the loser-spouse. 
 
Exclusive property of each spouse (Arts. 
109-115) 
 
1. Those brought to the marriage as his or 
her own; 
2. That which each acquires during the 
marriage by gratuitous title; 
 
Pensions will not form part of the conjugal 
partnership of gains when it is given to him 
voluntarily, and he is not entitled as a matter 
of right such as a fruit of industry or labor. 
 
Proceeds of a life insurance policy will not 
form part of the conjugal partnership of gains 
when the beneficiary of the life insurance is 
the estate, and the premiums are sourced 
from the separate property of the spouse. 
 
Retirement benefits will not form part of the 
conjugal partnership of gains when it is given 
to him voluntarily, and he is not entitled as a 
matter of right such as a fruit of industry or 
labor. 
 
3. That which is acquired by right of 
redemption, by barter, or by exchange 
with property belonging to only one of the 
spouses; and that which is purchased with 
exclusive money of the wife or of the 
husband (Art. 109, FC). 
 
Note: In right of redemption, for it to form 
part of the exclusive property of the spouse, 
the ownership over such property must still 
pertain to the said spouse. 
 
4. The amount of principal which may be 
collected during the marriage in partial 
payments or by installments on the 
amount of credit belonging to one of the 
spouses. 
 
XPN: Interests falling due during the 
marriage on the principal shall belong 
to the conjugal partnership (Art. 119, 
FC). 
 
The 
spouses 
retain 
the 
ownership, 
possession, administration, and enjoyment 
of their exclusive properties (Art. 110, FC). 
 
Rule on transfer of exclusive property 
under CPG 
 
1. Either spouse may, during the marriage, 
transfer the administration of his or her 
exclusive property to the other by means 
of a public instrument, which shall be 
recorded in the registry of property of the 
place the property is located (Art. 110, 
FC). 
2. A spouse may mortgage, encumber, 
alienate, or otherwise dispose of his or her 
exclusive property without the consent of 
the other spouse and appear alone in 
court to litigate with regard to the same 
(Art. 111, FC). 
3. The alienation of any exclusive property of 
a spouse administered by the other 
automatically 
terminates 
the 
administration over such property, and the 


<!-- PAGE 57 -->

 
 
48 
proceeds of the alienation shall be turned 
over to the owner spouse (Art. 112, FC). 
 
The settled rule is that the sale or 
encumbrance of a conjugal property requires 
the consent of both the husband and the wife 
(Guiang vs. CA, G.R. No. 127172, June 26, 
1998). 
 
The absence of the consent of one renders 
the entire sale or encumbrance null and void, 
including the portion of the conjugal property 
pertaining to the husband who contracted the 
sale. Neither would the conjugal partnership 
be liable for the loan on the ground that it 
redounded to the benefit of the family. The 
sweeping conclusion that the loan was 
obtained by the husband in order to finance 
the construction of housing units, without, 
however, adducing adequate proof, does not 
persuade (Homeowners Savings & Loan 
Bank vs. Dailo, G.R. No. 153802, March 11, 
2005). 
 
Rules on donation of exclusive property 
 
1. Property donated or left by will to the 
spouses, jointly and with designation of 
determinate shares, shall pertain to the 
donee-spouses as his or her own 
exclusive property, and in the absence 
of designation, share and share alike, 
without prejudice to the right of accretion 
when proper (Art. 113, FC). 
2. If the donations are onerous, the amount 
of the charges shall be borne by the 
exclusive property of the donee spouse, 
whenever they have been advanced by 
the conjugal partnership of gains (Art. 
114, FC). 
3. Retirement 
benefits, 
pensions, 
annuities, gratuities, usufructs, and 
similar benefits shall be governed by the 
rules 
on 
gratuitous 
or 
onerous 
acquisitions as may be proper in each 
case (Art. 115, FC). 
 
 
 
 
Ownership of property bought through 
installment 
 
Property bought on installments paid partly 
from exclusive funds of the spouses and 
partly from conjugal funds: 
 
1. If 
full 
ownership 
vested 
before 
marriage, it shall belong to the buyer 
spouse; 
2. If 
full 
ownership 
vested 
during 
marriage, it shall belong to the conjugal 
partnership. 
 
In either case, any amount advanced by the 
partnership or by either or both spouses shall 
be reimbursed by the owner or owners upon 
liquidation of the partnership (Art. 118, FC).
