VII. SUCCESSION 
 
 
A. DEFINITION – NCC, ARTS. 774 AND 
712 
 
Succession is a mode of acquisition by 
virtue of which the property, rights and 
obligations (PRO), to the extent of the value 
of the inheritance, of a person are 
transmitted through death to another or 
others either by will or by operation of law 
(Art. 774, Civil Code). 
 
Kinds of Succession 
 
1. Testamentary 
2. Legal or Intestate 
3. Mixed 
4. Compulsory 
2. Scope of Inheritance 
 
GR: 1. All the property, rights and obligations 
(to the extent of the inheritance) of a person 
which are not extinguished by such person’s 
death (Art. 776, Civil Code). 
 
2. The property and the transmissible rights 
and obligations which have accrued to item 
(1) above since the opening of the 
succession (Art. 781, Civil Code). 
 
XPN: Rights and obligations which are not 
transmissible (Art. 1311, Civil Code); 
 
Intransmissible by nature -refers to 
rights and obligations which are 
strictly personal. 
 
B. ELEMENTS 
 
1. Death – NCC, Arts. 775 and 777 
Rules on Opening of Succession 
 
The rights to succession are transmitted from 
the moment of the DEATH of the decedent. 
(Art. 777, Civil Code) 
 
A person may be “presumed” dead for the 
purpose of opening his succession after 
absence of: 
No. of Years  
Age of Disappearance 
10 years 
75 years old and below 
5 years 
76 years old and above 
 
A person who has gone missing for at 
least four (4) years: 
 
 
 
1. While on board a lost sea vessel or 
airplane; or 
 
2. While taking part in war as a member of 
the armed forces; or 
 
3. Has been in danger of death under other 
circumstances shall be presumed dead for 
the purpose of division of the estate. 
 
If the absent person appears, such person 
may recover the properties in the condition in 
which they may be found or the price of any 
alienated property may be given to said 
returning person (Arts. 390-392, Civil Code). 
The presumption of death under Article 390 
and 391 of the Civil Code arises by 
operation of law, without need of a court 
declaration. (Estrellita Tadeo-Matias vs. 
Republic of the Philippines, G.R. No. 
230751, 2018) 
 
2. Inheritance – NCC, Arts. 775 and 
781 
 
Article 775, Civil Code. "Decedent" is the 
general term applied to the person whose 
property is transmitted through succession, 
whether or not he left a will. If he left a will, 
he is also called the testator.  
 
Article 781, Civil Code. The inheritance of a 
person includes not only the property and the 


<!-- PAGE 159 -->

 
 
150 
transmissible rights and obligations existing at the 
time of his death, but also those which have 
accrued thereto since the opening of the 
succession.  
 
Relevant Rules 
 
1. The heir becomes the owner of his 
share in the inheritance as well as all fruits 
which accrue to such share upon the death 
of the decedent; 
 
2. Upon the death of the decedent, heirs may 
exercise 
their 
right 
to 
immediately 
possess, administer and dispose of their 
shares in the estate (in the absence of 
existing debts/claims against the estate); 
 
3. Since succession takes place by operation 
of law at the moment of the death of the 
decedent, the heirs can sue upon the 
rights of the decedent, without having to be 
appointed executor or administrator and 
without need of a judicial declaration of their 
status as heirs  
 
5. Heirs may also be sued without a 
previous declaration of heirship, provided 
there is no pending special proceeding for 
the settlement of estate of the decedent  
 
6. The possession of hereditary property is 
deemed transmitted to the heir without 
interruption and from the moment of 
death of the decedent, in case the 
inheritance is accepted (Art. 533(1), Civil 
Code); 
 
7. Estate taxes accrue upon death of the 
decedent, even if the heirs come into 
possession only later. 
 
3. Successors – NCC, Art. 782 
 
Article 782. An heir is a person called to the 
succession either by the provision of a will or by 
operation of law. 
 
Devisees and legatees are persons to whom gifts 
of real and personal property are respectively 
given by virtue of a will. 
 
HEIRS 
(Compulsory, Voluntary/Testamentary, 
Legal/Intestate) 
 
Kinds of Successors/ Heirs: 
 
1. Compulsory Heirs; 
 
Compulsory heirs are those who are 
entitled to receive a portion of the decedent’s 
estate, called the legitime, by operation of 
law. Article 887 of the Civil Code enumerates 
compulsory 
heirs, 
including 
legitimate 
children and descendants, legitimate parents 
and ascendants, the surviving spouse, and 
illegitimate children. Their legitime is a 
reserved portion that cannot be diminished 
except through lawful disinheritance under 
Article 919. In Reyes v. Tan (G.R. No. 
169264, January 27, 2006), the Supreme 
Court emphasized that compulsory heirs 
cannot be deprived of their legitime without 
valid cause. 
 
2. Voluntary or Testamentary Heir; 
 
Voluntary or testamentary heirs are those 
designated by the testator in their will to 
inherit specific portions of the estate not 
reserved for compulsory heirs. 
 
a. Instituted heir by universal title 
b. Devisees/Legatees 
 
Legatees are persons to whom gifts of 
personal property are given by virtue of a will 
(Art. 782, Civil Code) 
 
Devisees are persons to whom gifts of real 
property are given by virtue of a will (Art. 782, 
Civil Code) 
Legacy 
Devise 
A gift of personal 
property given in a 
will 
 
A gift of real 
property given in a 
will 
 


<!-- PAGE 160 -->

 
 
151 
It is bequeathed. 
It is devised. 
 
Instances when legacy/devise is deemed 
revoked 
 
a. Testator TRANSFORMS the thing such 
that it does not retain its original form or 
denomination. 
 
b. Testator ALIENATES the thing by any title 
or for any cause. Reacquisition of the thing 
by the testator does not make the legacy or 
devise valid, unless it is effected by the right 
of repurchase. 
 
c. The thing is TOTALLY LOST during the 
lifetime or after the death of the testator. 
 
d. Other causes. Nullity of will, non-
compliance with a suspensive condition, sale 
of the thing to pay the debts of the deceased 
during the settlement of his estate. 
 
3. Legal or Intestate Heirs. 
 
Legal or intestate heirs inherit under the 
rules of intestacy when a valid will is absent, 
incomplete, void, or fails to distribute the 
entire estate.  
 
Institution and substitution of heirs 
 
A will shall be valid even though it: 
 
1. Should not contain an institution of 
an heir; or 
2. 
Such 
institution 
should 
not 
comprise the 
entire estate; or 
3. The person so instituted should not 
accept 
the 
inheritance 
or 
be 
incapacitated to succeed. 
 
In such cases, the testamentary dispositions 
made in accordance with law shall be 
complied with and the remainder of the 
estate shall pass to the legal heirs (Art. 841, 
Civil Code). 
 
Institution of heirs 
 
Limitations on the Institution of Heirs 
 
1. Extent of Grant (Art. 842, Civil Code) . 
Freedom of disposition depends upon the 
existence, kind and number of compulsory 
Heirs. 
 
A. No compulsory heirs – Testator 
has full power of disposition  
 
B. 
With 
compulsory 
heirs 
– 
Testators cannot disregard the rights 
of the compulsory heirs. Testator may 
dispose of the free portion of his 
estate only.  
 
2. Effect of Predecease of Heir (Art. 856, 
Civil Code). Any heir who dies before the 
testator or is incapacitated to succeed or 
renounces the inheritance transmits no rights 
of the testator to his own heirs. 
 
Collective Institution 
 
GR: A collective institution of some heirs, 
when made in conjunction with the individual 
institution of other heirs, shall be deemed as 
an individual institution.  
 
XPN: Where it is clear that the testator 
intended to institute the heirs collectively, 
then the will of the testator shall be given 
effect. (Art. 847, Civil Code) 
 
The Civil Code contains no express 
proscription against the collective institution 
of heirs. However, Art. 847 provides for a 


<!-- PAGE 161 -->

 
 
152 
presumption against collective institutions, 
where it is made in conjunction with 
individual institutions of heirs.  
 
Manner of Distribution:  
 
1. Heirs instituted without designation of 
shares shall inherit in equal parts (Art. 846, 
Civil Code). 
 
2. If the institution pertains to some heirs 
individually and others collectively, the 
presumption is that all are individually 
instituted (Art. 847, Civil Code)  
 
3. If siblings are instituted (whether full or 
half-blood), the presumption is that the 
inheritance is to be distributed equally (Art. 
848, Civil Code).  
 
4. If parents and children are instituted, they 
are presumed to have been instituted 
simultaneously and not successively (Art. 
849, Civil Code) 
 
Declaration of Heirship, as decided in 
Treyes v. Larlar, G.R. No. 232579 (2020) 
 
The rule which requires a prior determination 
of heirship in a separate special proceeding 
as a prerequisite before one can file an 
ordinary civil action to enforce ownership 
rights acquired by virtue of succession, is 
abandoned.  
 
Henceforth, the rule is: unless there is a 
pending 
special 
proceeding 
for 
the 
settlement of the decedent’s estate or for the 
determination of heirship, the compulsory or 
intestate heirs may commence an ordinary 
civil action to declare the nullity of a deed or 
instrument, and for recovery of property, or 
any other action in the enforcement of their 
ownership rights acquired by virtue of 
succession, without the necessity of a 
prior and separate judicial declaration of 
their status as such.  
 
The ruling of the trial court shall only be in 
relation to the cause of action of the ordinary 
civil action, i.e., the nullification of a deed or 
instrument, and recovery or reconveyance of 
property, which ruling is binding only 
between and among the parties.  
 
Substitution of heirs 
 
Substitution is the appointment of another 
heir, so that he may enter into the inheritance 
in default of the heir originally instituted (Art. 
857, Civil Code). The substitute shall be 
subject to the same charges and conditions 
imposed upon the instituted heir, unless the 
testator has expressly provided the contrary, 
or the charges or conditions are personally 
applicable only to the heir instituted (Art. 862, 
Civil Code).  
 
a. Causes of Substitution:  
 
The testator may designate a substitute for 
any of the following causes:  
 
1. If the heir predeceases the 
testator;  
2. If the heir renounces or repudiates 
the inheritance; or  
3. If the heir becomes incapacitated 
to accept the inheritance (Art. 859, 
Civil Code).  
 
b. Kinds of Substitution:  
 
1. Brief or Compendious (Art. 860, Civil 
Code) 
 
Brief. Two (2) or more persons were 
designated 
by 
the 
testator 
to 
substitute for only one (1) heir.  
 
Compendious. One (1) person is 
designated to take the place of two 
(2) or more heirs.  
 
2. Reciprocal (Art. 861, Civil Code) 
 
If the heirs instituted in unequal shares 
should be reciprocally substituted, the 


<!-- PAGE 162 -->

 
 
153 
substitute shall acquire the share of the heir 
who dies, renounces, or is incapacitated, 
unless it clearly appears that the intention of 
the testator was otherwise.  
 
If there is more than one (1) substitute, they 
shall have the same share in the substitution 
as the institution.  
 
Example (only 1 substitute):  
 
If 
A 
and 
B 
are 
reciprocally 
substituted, and A dies before the 
testator, 
renounces, 
or 
is 
incapacitated, B acquires A’s share, 
regardless of whether their original 
shares were equal or unequal.  
 
Example (more than 1 substitute):  
 
A is instituted to 1/3, B to 1/6, and C to 1/2, with 
reciprocal substitution among them. 
 
If C dies before the testator, renounces, or is 
incapacitated, A and B inherit C’s share in 
proportion to their original shares. 
 
Thus, A receives twice as much as B, because 
A’s original share (1/3) is twice B’s (1/6). 
 
 
3. Simple Substitution (Art. 859, Civil 
Code) 
 
The testator may designate one (1) or more 
persons to substitute the heir/s instituted in 
case the heirs should:  
i. 
Die before him;  
ii. 
Should not wish to accept the 
inheritance 
(repudiation); 
or 
Should be incapacitated to accept 
the inheritance. 
4. Fideicommissary Substitution  
 
The testator institutes an heir with an 
obligation to preserve and to deliver to 
another the property so inherited. The heir 
instituted to such a condition is called the 
First Heir or the Fiduciary Heir; the one to 
receive the property is the Second Heir or the 
Fideicommissary (Art. 863, Civil Code).  
 
 
Requisites (Arts. 863-865, Civil Code) 
 
1. A Fiduciary or first heir instituted is 
entrusted with the obligation to preserve and 
to 
transmit 
to 
a 
Fideicommissary 
Substitute or second heir the whole or part 
of the inheritance.  
 
2. The substitution must not go beyond one 
(1) degree from the heir originally instituted. 
 
3. 
The 
Fiduciary 
Heir 
and 
the 
Fideicommissary are living at the time of the 
death of the testator.  
 
4. The fideicommissary substitution must be 
expressly made.  
 
5. The fideicommissary substitution is 
imposed on the free portion of the estate and 
never on the legitime.  
 
In the absence of an obligation on the part of 
the first heir to preserve the property for the 
second heir, there is no fideicommissary 
substitution (PCIB v. Escolin, G.R. Nos. L-
27860 and L-27896 (1974)). 
 
Example: 
 
T’s will: “I give my house to A, with the 
obligation to preserve it and deliver it 
to B upon A’s death.” 
 
Here, A is the fiduciary (first) heir, 
obliged to preserve the property, and 
B is the fideicommissary (second) 
heir who will receive it later. 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 163 -->

 
 
154 
Effects 
of 
predecease 
of 
the 
first 
heir/fiduciary 
or 
the 
second 
heir/fideicommissary:  
 
Situation 1: If the heir dies followed by the 
second heir, then the testator dies, who 
will inherit?  
 
The legal heirs. There is no fideicommissary 
substitution because first and second heirs 
are not living at the time of the testator’s 
death (Art. 863, Civil Code). 
 
To illustrate: 
 
Juan executed a will naming Maria as 
the first heir (fiduciary) and Pedro as 
the second heir (fideicommissary). 
Before Juan died, Maria and Pedro 
both predeceased him. Since neither 
the first heir nor the second heir was 
alive at the time of Juan’s death, the 
fideicommissary substitution failed. 
 
Situation 2: The testator dies first 
followed by the second heir. The first heir 
survived them but subsequently died, 
who will inherit?  
 
The second heir and his heirs under Art. 866, 
Civil Code. This is because the second heir 
passes his rights to his own heirs when he 
dies before the first heir. 
 
To illustrate: 
 
In his will, Juan named Maria as the 
first heir and Pedro as the second 
heir. Juan died in 2025. Pedro 
survived Juan but died shortly 
thereafter. Maria, the first heir, 
outlived Pedro but also passed away 
in 2026. 
 
Because the second heir (Pedro) 
survived the testator, he acquired the 
right 
to 
the 
fideicommissary 
substitution. Upon Pedro's death, his 
rights passed to his own heirs. 
Therefore, Pedro’s heir can inherit 
Juan’s estate under Article 866 of the 
Civil Code. 
 
Situation 3: If the first heir dies, followed 
by the testator, then the second heir, who 
will inherit?  
 
No specific provision in law, but the second 
heir inherits because the testator intended 
him to inherit. 
 
To illustrate: 
 
Juan named Maria as the first heir 
and Pedro as the second heir. Maria 
predeceased Juan. When Juan died, 
Pedro survived him and inherited the 
estate as the second heir. However, 
Pedro later passed away. 
 
Even though Maria (the first heir) was 
already deceased, Pedro, the second 
heir, inherited the estate upon the 
testator’s death. This was consistent 
with Juan’s intent for Pedro to 
succeed Maria. Pedro's estate was 
then inherited by his own legal heirs. 
 
Although 
there 
is 
no 
specific 
provision in the law addressing this 
scenario, the principle of honoring the 
testator’s intent allows Pedro to 
inherit. 
 
Dispositions with a condition or term 
 
A. Dispositions with a condition 
 
The institution of an heir may be made 
conditionally, or for a certain purpose or 
cause. (Article 871, Civil Code) 
 
Conditions imposed by the testator upon the 
heirs shall be governed by the rules 
established for conditional obligations in all 
matters not provided for by this Section (Art. 
884, NCC). 
 
GR: The testator is free to impose any 
condition, mode, or term on testamentary 
dispositions. 


<!-- PAGE 164 -->

 
 
155 
XPN: The testator cannot impose upon the 
legitime any burden, encumbrance, charge, 
term, condition, or substitution of any kind 
whatsoever. 
 
Note: Should he do so, the same 
shall be considered as not imposed. 
(Article 872, Civil Code) 
 
1. Impossible 
Conditions. 
Impossible 
conditions and those contrary to law or 
good customs shall be considered as not 
imposed and shall in no manner 
prejudice the heir, even if the testator 
should otherwise provide. (Article 873, 
Civil Code) 
 
2. An absolute condition not to contract 
a first or subsequent marriage. It shall 
be CONSIDERED AS NOT WRITTEN 
unless such condition has been imposed 
on the widow or widower by the 
deceased spouse, or by the latter's 
ascendants or descendants. (Article 874, 
Civil Code) 
 
3. Dispositions in favor of the testator -  
by the heir (Disposition Captatoria). 
Any disposition made upon the condition 
that the heir shall make some provision in 
his will in favor of the testator or of any 
other person shall be VOID. (Article 875, 
Civil Code) 
 
4. Modal Institution. It is the institution of an 
heir made for a certain purpose or cause 
(Arts. 881 and 882, NCC). While a condition 
suspends but does not obligate, a mode 
obligates but does not suspend. In case of 
doubt, the institution, devise, or legacy must 
be considered as modal (Jurado, Comments 
and Jurisprudence on Succession, 2009 ed., 
p. 229). 
 
In a modal institution, the testator states: 
 
(1) the object of the institution, 
(2) the purpose or application of the 
property left by the testator, or 
(3) the charge imposed by him upon 
the heir. 
As a rule, an obligation imposed upon an heir 
is not considered a condition unless it 
clearly appears that such was the intention of 
the testator. 
 
Effect. 
The 
instituted 
heir 
can 
claim 
immediate delivery of the inheritance subject 
to the giving of security or bond for 
compliance with the obligation. In case of 
failure to comply with the obligation, the 
instituted heir shall be compelled to return 
whatever they may have received by virtue of 
the institution, together with its fruits and 
interests (Rabuya, Civil Law Reviewer Vol. 1, 
2021, p. 1009). 
 
Kinds of Conditions 
Potestative 
Casual 
Mixed 
One which 
depends 
exclusively 
upon the will 
of the heir, 
devisee, or 
legatee, and 
must be 
performed by 
him 
personally. 
One that 
does not 
depend upon 
the will of the 
heir, devisee, 
or legatee, 
upon but the 
chance 
and/or upon 
the will of a 
third person. 
One that 
depends 
jointly upon 
the will of the 
heir, devisee, 
or legatee 
and upon 
chance 
and/or the 
will of a third 
person. 
 
B. Dispositions with a term 
 
The demandability or extinguishment of 
successional rights is made to depend upon 
the arrival of a day certain or upon the 
happening of a future but certain event. 
(Art. 871, NCC). 
 
A disposition with a suspensive term does 
not prevent the instituted heir from acquiring 
his rights and transmitting them to his heirs. 
(Article 878, Civil Code) 
 
 
 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 165 -->

 
 
156 
4. Acceptance (and Repudiation of 
Inheritance) – NCC, Arts. 1041-
1057 
 
Article 1041, Civil Code. The acceptance or 
repudiation of the inheritance is an act which 
is purely voluntary and free.  
 
Article 1042, Civil Code. The effects of the 
acceptance or repudiation shall always 
retroact to the moment of the death of the 
decedent.  
 
Article 1043, Civil Code. No person may 
accept or repudiate an inheritance unless he 
is certain of the death of the person from 
whom he is to inherit, and of his right to the 
inheritance.  
 
Article 1044, Civil Code. Any person having 
the free disposal of his property may accept 
or repudiate an inheritance. 
 
Any 
inheritance 
left 
to 
minors 
or 
incapacitated persons may be accepted by 
their parents or guardians. Parents or 
guardians may repudiate the inheritance left 
to their wards only by judicial authorization. 
 
The right to accept an inheritance left to the 
poor shall belong to the persons designated 
by the testator to determine the beneficiaries 
and distribute the property, or in their default, 
to those mentioned in article 1030.  
Article 
1045, 
Civil 
Code. The 
lawful 
representatives 
of 
corporations, 
associations, 
institutions 
and 
entities 
qualified to acquire property may accept any 
inheritance left to the latter, but in order to 
repudiate it, the approval of the court shall be 
necessary. 
 
Article 1046, Civil Code. Public official 
establishments can neither accept nor 
repudiate an inheritance without the approval 
of the government.  
 
Article 1047, Civil Code. A married woman of 
age may repudiate an inheritance without the 
consent of her husband.  
 
Article 1048, Civil Code. Deaf-mutes who 
can read and write may accept or repudiate 
the inheritance personally or through an 
agent.  
 
 
 
Should they not be able to read and write, the 
inheritance shall be accepted by their 
guardians. These guardians may repudiate 
the same with judicial approval.  
 
Article 1049, Civil Code. Acceptance may be 
express or tacit. 
Express 
Tacit 
An 
express 
acceptance must be 
made in a public or 
private document. 
 
 
A tacit acceptance is 
one 
resulting 
from 
acts by which the 
intention to accept is 
necessarily 
implied, 
or which one would 
have no right to do 


<!-- PAGE 166 -->

 
 
157 
except in the capacity 
of an heir. 
 
Acts of mere preservation or provisional 
administration do not imply an acceptance of 
the inheritance if, through such acts, the title 
or capacity of an heir has not been assumed.  
 
Article 1050, Civil Code. An inheritance is 
deemed accepted: 
 
(1) If the heirs sells, donates, or assigns 
(SAD) his right to a stranger, or to his co-
heirs, or to any of them; 
 
(2) If the heir renounces the same, even 
though gratuitously, for the benefit of one or 
more of his co-heirs; 
 
(3) If he renounces it for a price in favor of all 
his co-heirs indiscriminately; but if this 
renunciation should be gratuitous, and the 
co-heirs in whose favor it is made are those 
upon whom the portion renounced should 
devolve by virtue of accretion, the inheritance 
shall not be deemed as accepted.  
 
Article 1051, Civil Code. The repudiation of 
an inheritance shall be made in a public or 
authentic 
instrument, 
or 
by 
petition 
presented to the court having jurisdiction 
over 
the 
testamentary 
or 
intestate 
proceedings.  
 
 
Article 1052, Civil Code. If the heir repudiates 
the inheritance to the prejudice of his own 
creditors, the latter may petition the court to 
authorize them to accept it in the name of the 
heir. 
 
The acceptance shall benefit the creditors 
only to an extent sufficient to cover the 
amount of their credits. The excess, should 
there be any, shall in no case pertain to the 
renouncer, but shall be adjudicated to the 
persons to whom, in accordance with the 
rules established in this Code, it may belong.  
 
Article 1053, Civil Code. If the heir should die 
without having accepted or repudiated the 
inheritance his right shall be transmitted to 
his heirs.  
 
Article 1054, Civil Code. Should there be 
several heirs called to the inheritance, some 
of them may accept and the others may 
repudiate it.  
 
Article 1055, Civil Code. If a person, who is 
called to the same inheritance as an heir by 
will and ab intestato, 
repudiates the 
inheritance in his capacity as a testamentary 
heir, he is understood to have repudiated it in 
both capacities. 
 
Should he repudiate it as an intestate heir, 
without 
knowledge 
of 
his 
being 
a 
testamentary heir, he may still accept it in the 
latter capacity.  
 
Illustrative Example: 
 
A dies leaving a will naming X as heir. 
X is also A’s compulsory heir by law. 
If X repudiates the inheritance as a 
testamentary heir, he is deemed to 
have repudiated it both under the will 
and by intestacy. 
 
However, if X repudiates as an 
intestate heir, not knowing that he 
was also named in the will, X may still 
accept 
the 
inheritance 
as 
a 
testamentary heir once he learns of 
the will. 
 
Article 
1056, 
Civil 
Code. The 
acceptance or repudiation of an 
inheritance, 
once 
made, 
is 
IRREVOCABLE, and cannot be 
impugned, except when it was made 
through any of the causes that vitiate 
consent, or when an unknown will 
appears.  
 


<!-- PAGE 167 -->

 
 
158 
Article 1057, Civil Code. Within thirty days 
after the court has issued an order for the 
distribution of the estate in accordance with 
the Rules of Court, the heirs, devisees and 
legatees shall signify to the court having 
jurisdiction whether they accept or repudiate 
the inheritance. 
 
No right of representation when there is 
repudiation 
Heirs who repudiate 
their share may NOT 
be represented. (Art. 
977). 
 
Reyes v. Barretto-
Datu 
(G.R. 
No. 
160350, July 29, 2005), affirms this principle, 
holding that repudiation extinguishes the 
possibility 
of 
representation, 
thereby 
protecting the integrity of succession laws. 
For example, if a child renounces their share 
of an inheritance, their descendants cannot 
represent them and claim the repudiated 
share. 
 
Repudiation does NOT give rise to the 
Right of Representation 
Article 977. Heirs who repudiate their share 
may not be represented. 
Note: What is present is either 
accretion to the co-heirs or 
succession in one’s own right of the 
next heirs when accretion can no 
longer operate. 
Effect of Repudiation  
Article 1018. In legal succession the share 
of the person who repudiates the 
inheritance shall always accrue to his co-
heirs. 
 
 
 
Illustrative Sample - Accretion: 
A 
↓ 
B ®—— C —— D 
                     ↓ 
                E   &   F 
Estate of A: ₱300,000.00 
B, C, and D are called to the inheritance and 
would each be entitled to ₱100,000.00. 
B repudiates his share. 
Under Article 977, B cannot be represented by 
his children E and F. 
 
Accordingly, E and F inherit nothing. 
Under Article 1018, in legal (intestate) 
succession, the share of the heir who repudiates 
accrues to his co-heirs. 
Thus, C and D acquire B’s ₱100,000.00 share 
by accretion, in proportion to their respective 
shares. 
Final Distribution: 
C - ₱150,000.00 
D - ₱150,000.00 
E - ₱0 
F - ₱0 
 
 
DIFFERENT KINDS OF SUCCESSION 
(Testate & Intestate) 
 
C. TESTAMENTARY SUCCESSION 
 
a. Concept. It refers to the mode of 
succession established by the decedent in a 
will executed in accordance with the 
formalities and requirements prescribed by 
law. It allows a testator to exercise his right 
to control the disposition of his estate upon 
death, provided that the compulsory heirs are 
not deprived of their legitime. 


<!-- PAGE 168 -->

 
 
159 
b. Wills 
 
 
An act whereby a person is permitted, with 
the formalities prescribed by law to control to 
a certain degree the disposition of his estate 
to take effect after his death (Art. 783, dCivil 
Code). 
 
Characteristics of a will 
 
a. Purely personal. The making of a will is a 
strictly personal act. Thus, it cannot be left in 
whole or in part to the discretion of a third 
person, or it cannot be accomplished through 
an agent or attorney.  
 
GR: The following acts relative to making a 
will CANNOT be delegated to third persons: 
 
1. Designation of heirs, devisees, 
and legatees 
2. Duration/efficacy of designation 
3. Determination of portions, when 
4. Referred to by name [Art. 785, 
Civil Code].  
 
XPNs: The following acts relative to the 
making of a will CAN be delegated to third 
persons: 
 
1. Designation of person/institution 
falling 
under a class specified by testator 
 
2. Manner of distribution of property 
Specified by testator. 
 
b. Free and intelligent. Execution of a will 
tainted by any vices affecting the free will of 
the testator can cause its disallowance. 
 
c. Solemn or formal. If the formalities of a 
will required by law are not complied with, it 
will be disallowed. 
 
d. Revocable and ambulatory. A will can be 
revoked at any time before the testator’s 
death. 
 
e. Mortis causa. It takes effect upon the 
testator’s death. 
 
 
 
 
 
f. Individual. Prohibition against joint wills. 
 
Two or more persons cannot make a will 
jointly, or in the same instrument, either for 
their reciprocal benefit or for the benefit of a 
third person. 
 
Effect of Joint Wills Executed Abroad  
 
Wills executed by Filipinos in a foreign 
country shall not be valid in the Philippines, 
even though authorized by the laws of the 
country where they may have been executed 
(Art. 819, NCC).  
 
Note: Article 819 is an expression of public 
policy and is clearly one exception to the rule 
of lex loci celebrationis.  
 
However, the prohibition refers only to 
Filipinos. Hence, if made by foreigners 
abroad, and valid in accordance with Article 
816, the same should be considered as valid 
here. 
 
g. 
Executed 
with 
animus 
testandi. 
Intended by the testator to govern the 
disposition of testator's property upon 


<!-- PAGE 169 -->

 
 
160 
testator's death 
 
h. Executed with testamentary capacity  
 
i. Unilateral act. Does not involve an 
exchange of values or 
depend 
on 
simultaneous 
offer 
and 
acceptance. 
 
j. Dispositive. Even in the absence of 
dispositive provisions, a will disinheriting a 
compulsory heir is still a dispositive will 
because a disinheritance has the effect of 
disposing the legitime of the disinherited 
compulsory heir in favor of other compulsory 
heirs [Seangio v. Reyes, G.R. Nos. 140371-
72 (2006)]. 
 
XPN: Non-dispositive wills - example: a will 
recognizing an illegitimate child. 
 
k. Statutory grant. Permitted only by law, 
not a constitutional right. 
 
Requisites for Capacity to Make a Will 
 
1. The testator must not be expressly 
prohibited by law to make a will (Art. 796, 
Civil Code); 
 
2. The testator must be at least 18 years old 
(Art. 797, Civil Code); and 
 
3. The testator must be of sound mind at the 
time of execution (Art. 798, Civil Code). 
Test of soundness of mind 
 
To be of sound mind, the testator must know: 
 
1. The nature of the estate to be 
disposed of; 
2. The proper objects of his bounty; 
3. The character of the testamentary 
act (Art. 799, Civil Code) 
 
GR: Soundness of mind is presumed (Art. 
800, Civil Code). 
 
XPNs: 
1. When the testator, one (1) month or less 
before the execution of the will, was publicly 
known to be insane [Art. 800, Civil Code]. 
2. When the testator executed the will after 
being placed under guardianship or ordered 
committed, in either case, for insanity under 
Rules 93 and 101 of the Rules of Court, and 
before said order has been lifted (Torres v. 
Lopez, G.R. No. L-25966, 1926). 
Kinds of wills: Notarial and Holographic 
wills 
A. Notarial Will 
 
Requisites of a valid notarial will 
 
SPAAM 
 
1. Subscribed to by the testator or testator’s 
representative at the end; 
2. Pagination in letters; and 
 
3. Acknowledged before a notary public. 
 
4. Attested and subscribed by three (3) or 
more witnesses in the presence of the 
testator and of one another; 
 
Attestation Clause. Attested and 
subscribed by three (3) or more 
credible witnesses in the presence of 
the testator and of one another [Art. 
805,Civil Code] 
 
The attestation clause shall state the 
following: 
 
1. NUMBER of pages; 
 


<!-- PAGE 170 -->

 
 
161 
2. The FACT that the testator or his 
representative under his express 
direction signed the will and every 
page in the presence of instrumental 
witnesses; and 
 
3. That the witnesses signed the will 
and ALL its pages in the presence of 
the testator and of one another. 
 
5. Marginal signatures; 
 
Relevant Notes 
 
The signatures of the witnesses must be at 
the BOTTOM of the attestation clause 
(Cagro v. Cagro, G.R. No. L-5826, 1953).  
 
The notary public CANNOT be counted as 
an attesting witness (Cruz v. Villasor, G.R. 
No. L-32213, 1973). 
 
Test of presence 
 
Not whether they saw 
each other sign, but 
whether they might 
have seen each other 
sign had they chosen 
to do so considering 
their 
mental 
and 
physical condition and 
position with relation to each other at the 
moment of inscription of each signature 
[Jaboneta v. Gustilo, G.R. No. 1641 (1906)]. 
 
Effect of omissions. Omissions which can 
be supplied by an examination of the will 
itself, without the need of resorting to 
extrinsic evidence, will not be fatal and will 
not prevent allowance of the will. 
 
Marginal Signatures  
 
GR: Testator or his representative shall write 
his name, and the witnesses shall sign every 
page except the last page [Art. 805, Civil 
Code]. 
 
 
 
XPNs: 
 
1. When the will consists of only one (1) 
page; 
 
2. When the will consists of only two (2) 
pages, the first of which contains all 
dispositions and is signed at the bottom by 
the testator and the witnesses, and the 
second page contains only the attestation 
clause duly signed at the bottom by the 
witnesses (Abangan v. Abangan, G.R. No. L-
13431, 1919); 
 
3. The use of thumbprint was allowed 
(Matias v. Salud, G.R. No. L-10751, 1958); 
 
4. The inadvertent failure of one witness to 
affix his signature to one (1) page of a 
testament, due to the simultaneous lifting of 
two (2) pages in the course of signing, is not 
per se sufficient to justify denial of probate 
(Icasiano v. Icasiano, G.R. No. L-18979, 
1964). 
 
Page Numbers. All the pages of the will shall 
be numbered correlatively in letters placed 
on the upper part of each page [Art. 805, Civil 
Code]. 
 
Acknowledged before a Notary Public. 
The certification of acknowledgement need 
not be signed by the notary in the presence 
of the testator and the witnesses [Javellana 
v. Ledesma, G.R. L7179 (1955)]. 
 
Rule on Substantial Compliance 
 
In the ABSENCE of bad faith, forgery, or 
fraud (BFF), or undue and improper (UI) 
pressure and influence (PI), DEFECTS AND 
IMPERFECTIONS in the form of attestation 
or in the language used therein shall not 


<!-- PAGE 171 -->

 
 
162 
render the will invalid if it is PROVED that 
the will was in fact executed and attested in 
substantial compliance with ALL the 
requirements of article 805. (Article 809, Civil 
Code) 
 
Witnesses to a Notarial Will  
 
i. Qualifications (Art. 820, Civil Code) 
 
1. Of sound mind; 
2. Aged 18 years or over; 
3. Not blind, deaf or dumb; and 
4. Able to read and write. 
 
ii. Disqualifications (Art. 821, Civil Code) 
 
1. Person not domiciled in the 
Philippines; 
2. Those who have been convicted 
of falsification, perjury, or false 
testimony. 
 
Special requirements 
 
Additional requirements for Handicapped 
testators  
 
1. Deaf/Deaf-Mute. 
The 
testator must 
personally read the will if he is able to do 
so; or the Testator shall personally 
designate two (2) persons to read the 
contents and communicate it to him in 
some practicable manner. 
 
2.  Blind. The will shall be read to the 
testator twice by one of the subscribing 
witnesses and by the notary public 
acknowledging the will. 
 
A testator suffering from glaucoma may 
be considered as legally blind (Garcia v. 
Vasquez) 
 
The provision requiring reading of a will 
twice to a blind testator also applies to an 
illiterate testator (In Re: Probate of the 
Will of Cosico) 
 
B. Holographic Will 
Requisites of a valid holographic will: 
 
1. In writing. 
2. In a language known to the testator. 
3. Entirely written, dated and signed in the 
hand of the testator himself. 
 
 
Witnesses Required for Probate (Art. 
811, Civil Code) 
 
1. At least one (1) witness who knows the 
handwriting and signature of the testator; 
explicitly declare that it is the testator’s; 
2. If contested – at least three (3) of such 
witnesses; 
3. In the absence of a competent witness, 
expert testimony may be resorted to. 
 
GR: The holographic will itself must be 
presented for probate (Gan v. Yap, G.R. No. 
L-12190, 1958). 
 
XPN: If there is a photostatic copy or xerox 
copy of the holographic will, it may be 
presented for probate (Rodelas v. Aranza, 
G.R. No. L-58509 , 1982). 
 
Additional Dispositions 
 
In holographic wills, the dispositions of the 
testator written below his signature must be 
dated and signed by him in order to make 
them valid as testamentary dispositions [Art. 
812, Civil Code]. 
 
When a number of dispositions appearing in 
a holographic will are signed without being 
dated, and the last disposition has a 
signature and date, such date validates the 
dispositions preceding it, whatever be the 
time of prior dispositions [Art. 813, Civil 
Code]. 


<!-- PAGE 172 -->

 
 
163 
Insertion, 
Cancellation, 
Erasure 
or 
Alteration (Art. 814, Civil Code) 
 
To be valid, the testator must authenticate 
any insertion, cancellation, erasure, or 
alteration by his full signature. If such change 
is not authenticated by the testator, such 
change is considered not made. Thus, “the 
will is not thereby invalidated as a whole, but 
at most only as regards the particular words 
erased, corrected, or inserted” (Kalaw v. 
Relova, G.R. No. L-40207 (1984), citing 
Velasco v. Lopez, G.R. No. 905 (1903). 
 
Note: However, in the case of Kalaw v. 
Relova, 
the 
alteration 
involved 
the 
designation of the testator’s sole heir. In this 
case, the holographic will had only one (1) 
substantial provision, which was altered by 
substituting the original heir with another, but 
which alteration was not authenticated by the 
full signature of the testator, the effect must 
be that the entire will is voided or revoked for 
the simple reason that nothing remains in the 
will after that which could remain valid. 
 
Codicils and doctrine of incorporation by 
reference 
Codicil 
 
a. It is a supplement or addition to a will; 
b. Made after the execution of a will; 
c. Annexed to be taken as a part of the will; 
d. By which any disposition made in the 
original will is explained, added to, or altered; 
 
Note: A codicil must be executed as 
in the case of a will for it to be 
effective (Art. 826, Civil Code), but 
need not take the same form as the 
will to which it will be attached. Thus, 
a holographic will may have a notarial 
codicil, and a notarial will may have a 
holographic codicil. 
 
Incorporation by Reference 
 
Requisites: 
 
1. The document or paper referred to in the 
will must be in existence at the time of the 
execution of the will; 
2. The will must clearly describe and identify 
the same, stating among other things the 
number of pages thereof; 
3. It must be identified by clear and 
satisfactory proof as the document or paper 
referred to therein; and 
4. It must be signed by the testator and the 
witnesses on each and every page, except in 
case of voluminous books of account or 
inventories. 
 
Note: Not available in holographic 
wills. 
 
Revocation of wills and testamentary 
dispositions 
 
 
 
A will may be revoked by the testator at any 
time BEFORE his death. (Art. 828, Civil 
Code) 
 
Modes 
 
1. By implication of law; 
 
a. Preterition [Art. 854, Civil Code] 
b. Legal separation [Art. 63, Family 
Code] 
c. Unworthiness to succeed [Art. 
1032, Civil Code] 
 
2. By the execution of a will, codicil or 
other writing executed as provided in the 
case of wills (may be total or partial); or 
 
3. By burning, tearing, canceling, or 
obliterating (BOTC) the will with the 
intention of revoking it, by the testator 
himself, or by some other person in his 
presence, and by his express direction. 


<!-- PAGE 173 -->

 
 
164 
 
The act contemplating revocation must be 
done at any time before the death of the 
testator. The right of revocation cannot be 
waived or restricted (Art. 828, Civil Code). 
 
Note: Even if a holographic will was 
not intended to be revoked, unless a 
xerox copy exists, it can no longer be 
proved. 
 
Presumption of revocation 
 
The presumption of revocation applies when 
a will known to have been executed and in the 
possession or control of the testator is: 
 
Lost or Missing. If the will cannot be found 
after the testator’s death despite diligent 
efforts, the law presumes it was revoked by 
the testator during their lifetime. 
 
In Azaola v. Singson (G.R. No. L-2010), the 
Court noted that where a will cannot be 
located and there is no evidence of accidental 
loss 
or 
fraudulent 
destruction, 
the 
presumption 
arises 
that 
the 
testator 
intentionally 
revoked 
the 
will. 
The 
presumption may be rebutted with clear 
evidence of loss or concealment by third 
parties. 
 
Destroyed Without Authorization. If the will 
was destroyed without the testator’s express 
direction, its contents may still be established 
during probate if the due execution, contents, 
and unauthorized destruction are proven 
under the Rules of Court. 
 
 
 
 
“Doctrine of Dependent Relative 
Revocation” 
 
A revocation made in a subsequent will shall 
take effect, even if the new will should 
become inoperative by reason of the 
incapacity of the heirs, devisees or legatees 
designated therein, or by their renunciation 
(Art. 832, CC). 
 
The rule is established that where the act of 
destruction is connected with the making of 
another will so as fairly to raise the inference 
that the testator meant the revocation of the 
old to depend upon the efficacy of the new 
disposition intended to be substituted, the 
revocation will be conditional and dependent 
upon the efficacy of the new disposition; and 
if, for any reason, the new will intended to be 
made as a substitute is inoperative, the 
revocation fails and the original will remains 
in full force.  
 
In Testate Estate of Mariano Molo (G.R. 
No. L-2538, September 21, 1951), the 
Supreme Court applied the Doctrine of 
Dependent Relative Revocation by holding 
that the revocation of the 1918 will was 
conditional upon the validity of the later 1939 
will. Although the 1939 will expressly revoked 
the earlier will, it was ultimately disallowed for 
non-compliance with legal formalities. The 
Court ruled that any revocation or destruction 
of the 1918 will was done under the mistaken 
belief that the 1939 will was valid and 
effective. Since the subsequent will failed, 
the condition for revocation likewise failed, 
and the 1918 will remained valid and was 
admitted to probate.

Articles 835 & 836, Civil Code 
 
Article 835. The testator cannot republish, 
without reproducing in a subsequent will, the 
dispositions contained in a previous one 
which is void as to its form.  
 
Article 836. The execution of a codicil

republishing the will as modified by the 
codicil.

If after making a will, the testator makes a 
second will expressly revoking the first, the 
revocation of the second will does not 
revive the first will, which can be revived 
only by another will or codicil. (Article 837, 
Civil Code) 
 
Allowance and disallowance of wills 
 
 
Probate is a proceeding in rem is required to 
establish the validity of a will and in order to 
pass real or personal property. The testator 
himself may, during his lifetime, petition the 
court having jurisdiction for the allowance of 
his will (Art. 838, Civil Code). 
 
Note: Probate of a will is MANDATORY. 
 
No will shall pass either real or personal 
property UNLESS it is proved and allowed 
in accordance with the Rules of Court (Art. 
838, Civil Code). 
 
Kinds of Probate 
 
1. Post-mortem: after death 
2. Ante-mortem: during the testator’s 
lifetime 
 
Important factors to determine in probate: 
 
1. Identity of the will 
2. Its due execution 
3. Testamentary capacity of the 
testator 
2. Scope of Probate Proceedings 
(Art. 839, Civil Code) 
 
GR: The probate court CANNOT inquire into 
the 
intrinsic 
validity 
of 
testamentary 
provisions. Only the extrinsic validity of such 
wills may be examined. 
 


<!-- PAGE 175 -->

 
 
166 
XPNs: 
 
1. When practical considerations demand 
that the intrinsic validity of the will be 
resolved: When the will is intrinsically void on 
its face (e.g., when there is clearly a 
preterition). 
 
2. The claimant and all the other parties 
having legal interest in the property consent, 
either 
expressly 
or 
impliedly, 
to 
the 
submission of the question of intrinsic validity 
to the court. 
 
3. Probate court may pass upon the title to a 
property, 
but 
such 
determination 
is 
provisional and not conclusive, and is subject 
to the final decision in a separate action to 
resolve title  
 
4. The probate court may decide on the 
ownership of a property when the estate 
contains only one (1) property to be 
adjudicated upon because it would be 
impractical otherwise  
 
Grounds for Denying Probate 
 
1. If the formalities required by law have not 
been complied with; 
 
2. If the testator was insane or otherwise 
mentally incapable of making a will at the 
time of its execution; 
3. If it was executed through force or under 
duress, or the influence of fear, or threats; 
 
4. If it was procured by undue and improper 
pressure and influence, on the part of the 
beneficiary or some other person; 
 
Philippine law recognizes the probate of wills 
executed by foreign nationals abroad. Under 
Article 819 of the Civil Code, a will executed 
outside the Philippines by a foreigner may be 
admitted to probate if it complies with the 
formalities prescribed by the law of the place 
where it was executed. Thus, where the 
testatrix is a naturalized American citizen 
who executed her will in California while 
residing 
there, 
Philippine 
courts 
must 
determine the validity of the will’s formal 
execution in accordance with California law. 
(Linda Kucskar v. Cosme Sekito, Jr., G.R. 
No. 237449, December 2, 2020). 
 
COMPULSORY SUCCESSION 
 
Compulsory succession refers to the legal 
mandate under Philippine law that reserves 
a portion of the decedent’s estate for 
compulsory heirs, who cannot be deprived of 
their inheritance except for causes of 
disinheritance expressly allowed by law. 
 
Compulsory Heirs (Article 887, Civil Code) 
 
1. Primary Compulsory Heirs. The primary 
compulsory heirs are the following: 
 
 Legitimate 
Children 
and 
their 
Descendants (LCDs), with respect to 
their 
legitimate 
parents 
and 
ascendants. 
 
2. Secondary Compulsory Heirs. The 
secondary compulsory heirs are the following 
(who succeed only in default of the primary 
compulsory heirs): 
 
 Legitimate Parents and Legitimate 
Ascendants (LPs and LAs), with 
respect to their legitimate children 
and descendants. 
 
3. Concurring Heirs. The concurring heirs 
are the following (who are not excluded by 
the primary or secondary compulsory heirs, 
nor do they exclude one another): 
 
 Surviving Spouse (SS) 
 Illegitimate 
Children, 
including 
acknowledged 
natural 
children, 
natural children by legal fiction, and 
other illegitimate children referred to 
in Article 287. (IC/ICDs) 
 
Note: In all cases involving illegitimate 
children, their filiation must be duly proved.  
 


<!-- PAGE 176 -->

 
 
167 
The father or mother of illegitimate children 
may inherit from them only to the extent and 
in the manner provided by the Civil Code.

Computation 
of 
the 
net 
hereditary 
estate/determination of the legitime 
 
Net Estate (basis of legitime) 
= Gross Estate − Deductions (except estate tax) 
 
Net Estate (for physical distribution) 
= Gross Estate − Deductions − Estate Taxes 
  
Consequence 
of 
impairment 
of 
the 
legitime 
 
The legitime is INVIOLABLE and cannot be 
impaired 
by 
the 
testator’s 
acts. 
Any 
testamentary disposition that exceeds the 
free portion of the estate is ineffective insofar 
as it prejudices the legitime and is reduced to 
the extent necessary to preserve the 
compulsory heirs’ reserved shares. 
 
The legitime cannot be diminished by any 
condition, 
mode, 
or 
disposition 
that 
contravenes the mandatory provisions of the 
Civil Code. The law operates to automatically 
reduce excessive testamentary provisions in 
order to protect the compulsory heirs. 
 
The protection of the legitime extends 
beyond wills. Donations inter vivos that 
exceed the free portion are subject to 
collation 
and 
reduction, 
so 
that 
the 
compulsory heirs ultimately receive their full 
legitime and the estate is distributed in 
accordance with law. 
 
Preterition 
 
Preterition refers to the complete and total 
omission of a compulsory heir from the 
testator’s inheritance without the heir’s 
express 
disinheritance 
(Morales 
vs. 
Olondriz, G.R. No. 198994, February 3, 
2016). 
 
The preterition or omission of one, some, or 
all of the compulsory heirs in the DIRECT 
LINE, whether living at the time of the 
execution of the will or born after the death of 
the testator, shall annul the institution of 
heir.  
 
However, devises and legacies shall remain 
valid insofar as they are not inofficious. If the 
omitted compulsory heirs die before the 
testator, the institution shall be effectual, 
without 
prejudice 
to 
the 
right 
of 
representation (Art. 854, NCC). 
 
Requisites 
 
a. The heir omitted must be a compulsory 
heir in the direct line, whether ascending 
or descending. (Rabuya, Pre-Bar Reviewer 
Civil Law, 2021, p. 288). 
 
Even if the surviving spouse is a compulsory 
heir, there is no preterition if she is omitted 
from the inheritance, as she is NOT in the 
direct line. 
 
Preterition applies to legally adopted 
children. Adoption gives the adopted person 


<!-- PAGE 178 -->

 
 
169 
the same rights and duties as a legitimate 
child, making him/her a legal heir. (Acain vs. 
Intermediate Appellate Court, G.R. No. L-
72706, October 27, 1987). 
 
All compulsory heirs, including illegitimate 
children and illegitimate parents, are covered 
(Tolentino, Civil Code of the Philippines, Vol. 
III, Succession, p. 189). 
 
b. The omission must be complete and 
total in such a way that the omitted heir 
must receive nothing at all from the 
testator by any title whatsoever (Jurado, 
Comments 
and 
Jurisprudence 
on 
Succession, 2009 ed., p. 174). 
 
Total omission occurs when the testator does 
not disinherit the heir but leaves them 
nothing of the hereditary estate, whether or 
not they are mentioned in the will (Tolentino, 
Civil Code of the Philippines, Vol. III, 
Succession, p. 188). 
 
The heir must NOT have received anything 
by way of: 
 
a. Testamentary succession 
b. Legacy or devise 
c. Donation inter vivos 
d. Intestacy 
If the heir was given even a minimal share, 
there is no preterition, and the heir may only 
request completion of their share (Reyes vs. 
Barreto-Datu, G.R. No. L-17818, January 25, 
1967). 
 
c. The compulsory heir omitted should 
survive the testator (Jurado, Comments 
and Jurisprudence on Succession, 2009 ed., 
p. 174). 
d. The omission must result from Mistake, 
Inadvertence, or Oversight (MIO).  
 
If 
intentional, 
it 
constitutes 
invalid 
disinheritance (Rabuya, Pre-Bar Reviewer 
Civil Law, 2021, p. 289). 
 
Effects of Preterition 
 
 The 
institution 
of 
heirs 
is 
ANNULLED. 
 Legacies and devises remain valid 
insofar as they are not inofficious (Art. 
854, NCC). 
 If they impair the legitime, they are 
merely reduced. 
 If the will does not institute any 
devisees or legatees, preterition 
results 
in 
TOTAL 
INTESTACY 
(Rabuya, Pre-Bar Reviewer Civil 
Law, 2021, p. 288, citing Morales vs. 
Olondriz). 
Jurisprudential example on preterition 
 
In one case, the Supreme Court declared 
that the respondents were preterited by the 
will of the deceased. 
 
 
Article 854 of the Civil Code defines 
preterition as the omission of one, some, 
or all of the compulsory heirs in the direct 
line, whether living at the time of 
execution of the will or born after the 
death of the testator. It shall annul the 
institution of heirs; but the devises and 
legacies shall be valid insofar as they are not 
inofficious. 
In his Will, the deceased bequeathed only 
one property (the Pico de Loro condominium 
unit) in favor of the respondents. All other 
properties were left to the petitioners. 
However, this could not be given effect 
considering that the condominium unit did 
not belong to him at the time of his death. In 
effect, respondents will receive nothing from 
the decedent as there are no other properties 


<!-- PAGE 179 -->

 
 
170 
bequeathed in their favor. Though they were 
named as compulsory heirs, the respondents 
were not assigned any part of the estate 
without being expressly disinherited, thus 
depriving them of their legitimes. (★Nelfa 
Delfin Trinidad, et al. v. Salvador G. 
Trinidad, 
et 
al., 
G.R. 
No. 
254695, 
December 6, 2023, as penned by J. 
Gaerlan) 
 
 
Disinheritance 
 
A compulsory heir may, in consequence of 
disinheritance, be deprived of his legitime, for 
causes expressly stated by law (Art. 915, 
Civil Code). 
 
Requisites 
 
 
1. It must be for a cause Designated by law; 
2. It must be Unconditional; and 
3. It must be made in a Valid will; 
4. It must be made Expressly, stating the 
cause in the will itself; 
5. It must be Total. 
6. The cause must be certain and True and 
must be proved by the interested heir if the 
disinherited heir should deny it. 
 
Note: Heir disinherited must be 
designated by name or in such a 
manner as to leave no room for doubt 
as to who is intended to be 
disinherited. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Grounds for Disinheritance 
 
Causes Common to Disinheritance and 
Unworthiness 
 
Art. 
1032 
enumerates 
the 
acts 
of 
unworthiness which LIKEWISE constitute 
grounds to disinherit a compulsory heir.  
 
(1) Parents who have ABANDONED their 
children or induced their daughters to lead a 
corrupt or immoral life, or attempted against 
their virtue; 
 
(2) Any person who has been convicted of an 
ATTEMPT AGAINST THE LIFE of the 
testator, his or her spouse, descendants, or 
ascendants; 
 
(3) Any person who has accused the testator 
of a crime for which the law prescribes 
IMPRISONMENT for six years or more, if the 
accusation has been found groundless; 
 
(4) Any heir of full age who, having 
knowledge of the violent death of the 
testator, should FAIL TO REPORT it to an 
officer of the law within a month, unless the 
authorities have already taken action; this 
prohibition shall not apply to cases wherein, 
according to law, there is no obligation to 
make an accusation; 
 
(5) Any person convicted of ADULTERY OR 
CONCUBINAGE with the spouse of the 
testator; 
 
(6) Any person who by FRAUD, VIOLENCE, 
INTIMIDATION, OR UNDUE INFLUENCE 
should cause the testator to make a will or to 
change one already made; 
 
(7) Any person who by the same means 
PREVENTS another from making a will, or 
from revoking one already made, or who 
supplants, conceals, or alters the latter's will; 


<!-- PAGE 180 -->

 
 
171 
 
(8) Any person who FALSIFIES OR 
FORGES a supposed will of the decedent.  
 
 
Effects of disinheritance 
 
The effect of total exclusion from the 
inheritance is the loss of the legitime, the 
right to intestate succession, and any 
disposition in a prior will. 
 
Disinheritance, 
however, 
is 
without 
prejudice to the right of representation of 
the children and descendants of the person 
disinherited. But the disinherited parent shall 
not have the usufruct or administration of the 
property constituting the legitime (Art. 923, 
NCC). 
 
Effect of Disinheritance Without Cause 
 
Disinheritance without a specification of the 
cause, or for a cause the truth of which, if 
contradicted, is not proved, or which is not 
one of those set forth in this Code, shall 
annul the institution of heirs insofar as it 
may prejudice the person disinherited. 
However, the devises, legacies, and other 
testamentary dispositions shall be valid to 
such extent as will not impair the legitime 
(Art. 918, NCC). 
 
Right of Representation in Case of 
Disinheritance 
 
The causes of disinheritance are personal to 
the disinherited heir. Hence, in case of valid 
disinheritance, only the disinherited heir is 
deprived of his right to the legitime. However, 
the 
children 
or 
descendants 
of 
the 
disinherited heir can take his place and 
preserve the disinherited heir’s share to the 
legitime. 
 
Effect of reconciliation 
 
1. If no disinheritance has been made 
yet, the offended person will be deprived 
of his right to disinherit. 
 
2. If disinheritance has been effected, it 
will be rendered ineffectual. 
 
Effects 
of 
Setting 
Aside 
the 
Disinheritance 
 
1. The disinherited heir is restored to his 
legitime. 
2. If the disinheriting will did not dispose 
of 
the 
disposable 
portion, 
the 
disinherited heir is entitled to his 
proportionate share, if any, of the 
disposable portion. 
3. If the disinheriting will or any 
subsequent will disposed of the 
disposable 
portion 
in 
favor 
of 
testamentary heirs, legatees, or 
devisees, such dispositions remain 
valid.

D. INTESTATE SUCCESSION – NCC, ARTS. 
960-1014 
 
Legal or Intestate Succession - that which 
takes place if a person dies without a will, or 
with a void will, or one which has 
subsequently lost its validity. 
 
Intestate succession can take place even if 
there is a will, such as when the will does not 
dispose of all the disposable property of the 
decedent. In such case, the will defaulted 
pertains to the remaining property not 
covered by it. 
 
Causes of intestacy 
 
1. There is no will, the will is void, or 
the will is revoked. 
2. The will does not institute an heir 
to, or dispose of, all the property of 
the testator (partial intestacy). 
3. The suspensive condition attached to 
the inheritance does not happen or 
is not fulfilled. 
4. The heir predeceases the testator or 
repudiates the inheritance, and no 
substitution or right of accretion takes 
place. 
5. The heir instituted is incapacitated to 
succeed (Art. 960, NCC). 
 
Note: The enumeration is not exclusive; 
there are other causes for intestacy that are 
not included in the list. 
 
Examples: (TRIP) 
 
1. When a testamentary disposition is 
subject to a Term or period, and such 
term or period expires. 
2. When a testamentary disposition is 
subject to a Resolutory condition, and 
such condition is fulfilled. 
3. When a testamentary disposition is 
Impossible of compliance or is 
ineffective (Jurado, 2009) 
4. When there is a Preterition in the 
testator’s will. 
Order of intestate succession 
 
Order of Preference Between Lines 
 
1. First, succession takes place in the 
direct descending line (Art. 978, 
NCC). 


<!-- PAGE 182 -->

 
 
173 
2. Second, in the direct ascending line 
(Art. 985, NCC). 
3. Finally, in the collateral line (Art. 
1003, NCC). 
 
Order of Intestate Succession to a 
Legitimate Child. In general, and without 
prejudice to the concurrent right of other 
heirs in proper cases, the order of intestate 
succession to a legitimate child is as follows: 
 
1. Legitimate children and descendants. 
2. Legitimate parents and ascendants. 
3. Illegitimate children. 
4. Illegitimate parents. 
5. The surviving spouse. 
6. Brothers, sisters, nephews, and 
nieces. 
7. Other collaterals up to the fifth 
degree. 
8. The State (Rabuya, 2009; Balane, 
2016). 
Order of Intestate Succession to an 
Illegitimate Child 
1. The legitimate children and descendants 
of a person who is an illegitimate child are 
preferred over other intestate heirs, 
without 
prejudice 
to 
the 
right 
of 
concurrence of illegitimate children and 
the surviving spouse. 
2. In the absence of legitimate children and 
descendants, the illegitimate children (of 
the 
illegitimate 
child) 
and 
their 
descendants succeed to the entire 
estate, 
without 
prejudice 
to 
the 
concurrent right of the surviving spouse. 
3. In 
the 
absence 
of 
children 
and 
descendants, 
whether 
legitimate 
or 
illegitimate, the third in the order of 
succession 
to 
the 
estate 
of 
the 
illegitimate 
child 
is 
his 
illegitimate 
parents. 
 
If both parents survive and are entitled to 
succeed, they divide the estate share 
and share alike. 
4. Although the law is silent, if the surviving 
spouse of the illegitimate child concurs 
with the illegitimate parents, the surviving 
spouse shall be entitled to one-half of the 
estate, while the illegitimate parents get 
the other half. 
Note: In the ascending line, only the 
illegitimate parents are entitled to inherit 
from 
the 
illegitimate 
child; 
other 
illegitimate ascendants are not so 
entitled. 
5. In default of children or descendants, 
legitimate or illegitimate, and illegitimate 
parents, the surviving spouse shall inherit 
the entire estate. 
6. If the surviving spouse should survive 
with brothers and sisters, nephews, and 
nieces, the surviving spouse shall inherit 
one-half of the estate, and the latter the 
other half. 
7. The brothers and sisters must be by 
illegitimate filiation; otherwise, the Iron 
Curtain Rule shall apply. 
8. Although the law is silent, illegitimate 
brothers and sisters who survive alone 
shall get the entire inheritance. 
9. The legitimate children of the illegitimate 
parents are not entitled to inherit from the 
illegitimate child by virtue of Art. 992 of 
the NCC. 
10. The State. 
Rules on Exclusion and Concurrence in 
Intestate Succession 
1. Legitimate children  
a. Exclude parents, collaterals and State;  
b. Concur with surviving spouse and 
illegitimate children; and 
c. Excluded by no one. 
 
 


<!-- PAGE 183 -->

 
 
174 
2. Illegitimate children  
 
a. Exclude illegitimate parents, collaterals 
and State;  
b. Concur with surviving spouse, legitimate 
children, and legitimate parents; and  
c. Excluded by no one. 
 
 
3. Legitimate parents  
 
a. Exclude collaterals and the State;  
b. Concur with illegitimate children and 
surviving spouse; and  
c. Excluded by legitimate children. 
 
 
4. Illegitimate parents  
 
a. Exclude collaterals and State;  
b. Concur with surviving spouse; and  
c. Excluded by legitimate children and 
illegitimate children. 
 
 
5. Surviving spouse  
 
a. Excludes collaterals other than brothers, 
sister, nephews and nieces, and State;  
b. 
Concurs 
with 
legitimate 
children, 
illegitimate 
children, 
legitimate 
parents, 
illegitimate 
parents, 
brothers, 
sisters, 
nephews and nieces; and  
c. Excluded by no one. 
 
 
6. Brothers and Sister, nephews, and 
nieces  
 
a. Exclude all other collaterals and the State; 
b. Concur with surviving spouse; and  
c. 
Excluded 
by 
legitimate 
children, 
illegitimate children, legitimate parents and 
illegitimate parents. 
 
 
7. Other collaterals  
 
a. Exclude collaterals in remoter degrees and 
the State;  
b. Concur with collaterals in the same 
degree; and  
c. 
Excluded 
by 
legitimate 
children, 
illegitimate 
children, 
legitimate 
parents, 
illegitimate 
parents, 
surviving 
spouse, 
brothers and sisters, and nephews and 
nieces. 
 
8. State  
 
a. Excludes no one;  
b. Concurs with no one; and  
c. Excluded by everyone. (Balane, 2016)

Governing Rules in Intestate Succession 
 
1. Rule of Proximity (Article 962). In 
every inheritance, the relative nearest 
in degree excludes the more distant 
ones, 
saving 
the 
right 
of 
representation when it properly takes 
place. 
 
2. Rule of Equality of Degrees (Article 
962). Relatives in the same degree 
shall inherit in equal shares, subject to 
the provisions of: 
 
a. Art. 1006 with respect to relatives of 
the full/half blood. 
b. Art. 987 par. 2, concerning division 
between the paternal and maternal 
lines. 
 
3. Rule on Degrees of Relationship 
(Article 963). Proximity of relationship 
is determined by the number of 
generations. Each generation forms a 
degree. 
 
4. Rule on Lines of Relationship (Article 
964). A series of degrees forms a line, 
which may be either direct or 
collateral. 
 
5. Direct Line (Article 965). The direct 
line is that constituted by the series of 
degrees 
among 
ascendants 
and 
descendants. 
 
6. Collateral Line (Article 965). The 
collateral line is that constituted by the 
series of degrees among persons who 
are not ascendants or descendants, 
but who come from a common 
ancestor. 
 
7. Computation of Degrees in the Direct 
Line (Article 966). In the direct line, the 
number of degrees is equal to the 
number of generations. 
 
8. Computation of Degrees in the 
Collateral Line (Article 966). In the 
collateral line, the number of degrees 
is equal to the number of generations, 
counting from the common ancestor. 
 
9. Rule on Full Blood and Half Blood 
(Article 967). Full blood relationship is 
that existing between persons who 
have the same father and the same 
mother; half-blood relationship is that 
existing between persons who have 
only one of the same parents. 


<!-- PAGE 186 -->

 
 
177 
 
10. Rule on Accretion Among Relatives of 
the Same Degree (Article 968). If 
there are several relatives of the same 
degree, and one or some of them are 
unwilling or incapacitated to succeed, 
his portion shall accrue to the others 
of the same degree, save the right of 
representation when it should take 
place. 
 
11. Effect of Repudiation by the Nearest 
Relative 
(Article 
969). 
If 
the 
inheritance is repudiated by the 
nearest relative, the next in degree 
shall succeed. 
 
12. Right of Representation (Article 970). 
Representation is a right created by 
fiction of law, by virtue of which the 
representative is raised to the place 
and the degree of the person 
represented, and acquires the rights 
which the latter would have had if he 
were living or if he could have 
inherited. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Illustrative 
Examples 
on 
Right 
of 
Representation [ROR] (Article 970). In case 
of predecease, incapacity, or disinheritance, 
representation takes place. 
 
 
 
1. Representation in Case of Incapacity 
 
 
A 
↓      ↓     ↓ 
B(i)    C    D 
↓ 
E & F 
 
ESTATE OF A - ₱300,000 
 
B, C, and D are instituted as heirs and would 
receive ₱100,000 each. 
 
However, B is incapacitated. 
 
By right of representation, E and F will 
represent B and succeed to the entire share 
of B. 
 
Distribution: 
 
 E - ₱50,000 
 F - ₱50,000 
 C - ₱100,000 
 D - ₱100,000 
 
Note: Accretion does not apply because 
representation takes place. 
 
 
 
2. Representation in Case of Predecease 
 
 
A 
↓ 
†B    †C    †D 
   ↓       ↓      ↓

ESTATE OF A - ₱300,000 


<!-- PAGE 187 -->

 
 
178 
B, C, and D predeceased A. 
By right of representation, their respective 
descendants inherit per stirpes, each taking 
the share of the person represented.

Each receives ₱100,000, corresponding to
